\section{Critical reasoning formats}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Statement and assumption:} an assumption is what must be taken for granted for the statement to make sense. ``Use Brand X for whiter teeth'' assumes people want whiter teeth, but it does not assume that other brands fail.
\item \textbf{Statement and conclusion:} a conclusion must follow from the statement alone, without outside facts. Beware absolute words like ``all'', ``only'' and ``best''.
\item \textbf{Course of action:} a course of action must be practical and must address the stated problem, without being extreme.
\item \textbf{Cause and effect:} decide which event logically comes first, or if both have a common cause.
\end{itemize}

\section{Weighing puzzles}
\begin{idea}[Information counting]
A balance has \textbf{three outcomes} (left heavy, right heavy, balanced), so $k$ weighings distinguish at most $3^k$ cases.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
\item One heavy coin among $N$: split into three groups. $3^k$ coins need $k$ weighings.
\item One odd coin of unknown weight direction: at most $\frac{3^k-3}2$ coins, so 12 coins in 3 weighings.
\end{itemize}
\end{idea}

\begin{example}[8 coins, one heavy]
What is the minimum number of weighings on a balance?

\textbf{Solution.} $3^1<8\le3^2$, so at least 2. It is achieved by weighing 3 vs 3. If they balance, compare the remaining 2. Otherwise take the heavier 3 and weigh 1 vs 1. \textbf{2 weighings.}
\end{example}

\textbf{Measuring with weights:} with weights on one pan only, use powers of 2 (1, 2, 4, 8, \dots), which is binary. With weights allowed on both pans, use powers of 3 (1, 3, 9, 27, \dots), since each weight can be on the left, on the right, or unused. This is called balanced ternary.

\begin{exercises}
\item ``If it rains, the match is cancelled. The match was not cancelled.'' What can you conclude?
\item ``If it rains, the match is cancelled. The match was cancelled.'' Can you conclude it rained?
\item Write the negation of ``All bankers are rich''.
\item A says, ``B is a knave.'' B says, ``A and I are the same type.'' What are they?
\item Exactly one of these is true. I: ``Exactly one of these statements is false.'' II: ``Exactly two of these statements are false.'' III: ``All three are false.'' Which is true?
\item Minimum weighings to find a heavy coin among 27? Among 28?
\item What is the minimum number of weights to weigh every integer from 1 to 40 kg on one pan? With both pans usable?
\item Statement: ``The government has cut fuel tax to help consumers.'' Assumptions: I. Fuel prices affect consumers. II. Fuel tax is the only way to help consumers. Which is implicit?
\item ``No one gets in unless they have a ticket.'' Priya got in. What follows? Someone has a ticket; does it follow they got in?
\item Three boxes are labelled Apples, Oranges and Mixed, and every label is wrong. You may take one fruit from one box. Which box do you pick from, and how does it help?
\end{exercises}

%=====================================================================
\part{Probability and Quant Interviews}
%=====================================================================
\chapter{Foundations}\label{ch:found}

\section{The language}
An \textbf{experiment} has a \textbf{sample space} $\Omega$ of outcomes; an \textbf{event} is a subset. When outcomes are equally likely,
\[P(A)=\frac{|A|}{|\Omega|}\]
-- which is why Parts I and II matter: most aptitude probability is counting in disguise.

\begin{idea}[Rules you use constantly]
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
\item Complement: $P(\text{not }A)=1-P(A)$. ``At least one'' $\Rightarrow$ $1-P(\text{none})$.
\item Addition: $P(A\cup B)=P(A)+P(B)-P(A\cap B)$.
\item Conditional: $P(A\mid B)=\dfrac{P(A\cap B)}{P(B)}$; multiplication: $P(A\cap B)=P(B)\,P(A\mid B)$.
\item Independence: $P(A\cap B)=P(A)P(B)$, i.e.\ knowing $B$ doesn't change the chance of $A$.
\item Law of total probability: $P(A)=\sum_iP(A\mid B_i)P(B_i)$ over a partition $B_1,B_2,\dots$
\end{itemize}
\end{idea}

\begin{trap}[Common trap: mutually exclusive $\neq$ independent]
Mutually exclusive events (can't both happen) with positive probabilities are \emph{never} independent: if one happens, the other's probability drops to zero.
\end{trap}

\begin{trap}[Common trap: make outcomes equally likely]
``Two dice: the sum is 2, 3, \dots, 12, eleven outcomes, so $P(7)=\frac1{11}$'' is wrong. The 36 ordered pairs are equally likely; the sums are not. Always label the dice (red/blue) to count ordered outcomes.
\end{trap}

\begin{example}
Two dice are rolled. Probability that the sum is 7? That at least one shows a 6?

\textbf{Solution.} Sum 7: $(1,6),(2,5),\dots,(6,1)$ $\to\frac6{36}=\frac16$. At least one 6: $1-\left(\frac56\right)^2=\frac{11}{36}$.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[real life: two independent approvals]
A loan file needs approval from Credit (probability 0.8) and Compliance (0.9), decided independently. Probability it gets through? Probability that exactly one approves?

\textbf{Solution.} Both: $0.8\times0.9=0.72$. Exactly one: $0.8\times0.1+0.2\times0.9=0.26$.
\end{example}

\begin{exercises}
\item Two dice are rolled. What is the probability that the sum is a prime number?
\item From a bag of 5 red and 3 blue balls, two are drawn without replacement. Probability they are of different colours?
\item A 3-digit number is chosen at random. Probability that its digits are all distinct?
\item $P(A)=0.5$, $P(B)=0.4$, $P(A\cap B)=0.2$. Find $P(\text{neither})$. Are $A$ and $B$ independent?
\item A card is drawn from a standard deck. Probability it is a king or a heart?
\item Five people are chosen at random. Probability that at least two share a birth month (months equally likely)?
\end{exercises}

%=====================================================================
\chapter{Conditional Probability and Bayes' Theorem}\label{ch:bayes}

\section{Bayes' theorem}
\[P(H\mid E)=\frac{P(E\mid H)\,P(H)}{P(E\mid H)P(H)+P(E\mid \bar H)P(\bar H)}.\]
In words: \textbf{posterior $\propto$ likelihood $\times$ prior}. The denominator just rescales so the posteriors add to 1.

\begin{shortcut}[Speed shortcut: natural frequencies]
Imagine 10{,}000 people (or 1{,}000 items) and push them through the tree. Count, don't multiply fractions. This avoids almost every Bayes error and is how strong interview candidates talk through these problems.
\end{shortcut}

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[>={Latex},font=\scriptsize,scale=0.95]
\node[draw,rounded corners] (a) at (0,0) {10,000 people};
\node[draw,rounded corners] (s) at (3.4,1) {100 sick}; \node[draw,rounded corners] (h) at (3.4,-1) {9,900 healthy};
\node[draw,rounded corners,fill=red!10] (sp) at (7.2,1.5) {99 test $+$}; \node[draw,rounded corners] (sn) at (7.2,0.5) {1 test $-$};
\node[draw,rounded corners,fill=red!10] (hp) at (7.2,-0.5) {495 test $+$}; \node[draw,rounded corners] (hn) at (7.2,-1.5) {9,405 test $-$};
\draw[->] (a)--node[above,sloped]{1\%}(s); \draw[->] (a)--node[below,sloped]{99\%}(h);
\draw[->] (s)--node[above,sloped]{99\%}(sp); \draw[->] (s)--node[below,sloped]{1\%}(sn);
\draw[->] (h)--node[above,sloped]{5\%}(hp); \draw[->] (h)--node[below,sloped]{95\%}(hn);
\node[align=left,font=\small] at (11.1,0.5) {$P(\text{sick}\mid+)$\\[2pt] $=\frac{99}{99+495}$\\[2pt] $=\frac16$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}
\begin{example}[the false-positive paradox]
A disease affects 1\% of people. A test catches 99\% of true cases but also gives a positive result for 5\% of healthy people. You test positive. Probability you have the disease?

\textbf{Solution.} Of 10{,}000 people: 100 are sick, 99 test positive. 9{,}900 are healthy, 495 test positive. Total positives 594, of which 99 are sick:
\[P=\frac{99}{594}=\frac16\approx\mathbf{16.7\%}.\]
The low base rate dominates. This is the same logic as fraud-detection alerts in banking: most flagged transactions are false alarms when fraud is rare.
\end{example}

\section{Classic puzzles}
\begin{example}[Monty Hall]
Three doors: a car behind one, goats behind two. You pick door 1. The host, who \emph{knows} where the car is, opens another door showing a goat and offers a switch. Should you switch?

\textbf{Solution.} Your first pick is right with probability $\frac13$; then switching loses. With probability $\frac23$ your pick is wrong; the host is \emph{forced} to reveal the only other goat, and switching wins. Switch: $P(\text{win})=\mathbf{\frac23}$.
\end{example}

\begin{why}[Why it works: the host's knowledge carries the information]
The host's action depends on where the car is -- that's what makes it informative. If the host opened a door at random and it merely \emph{happened} to show a goat, switching would win only $\frac12$ of the time (see Exercise~\ref{ch:bayes}.5). With 100 doors and the host opening 98 goat doors, the intuition becomes obvious: switch.
\end{why}

\begin{example}[Bertrand's box]
Three boxes: gold--gold, silver--silver, gold--silver. You pick a box at random and draw a coin: it's gold. Probability the other coin in that box is gold?

\textbf{Solution.} Count gold \emph{coins}, not boxes: there are 3 gold coins, equally likely to be drawn, and 2 of them sit in the gold--gold box. Answer $\mathbf{\frac23}$ (not $\frac12$).
\end{example}

\begin{example}[the two-child problems]
A family has two children.
(a) At least one is a boy. Probability both are boys?
(b) At least one is a boy born on a Tuesday. Probability both are boys?

\textbf{Solution.} (a) Equally likely: BB, BG, GB, GG. ``At least one boy'' leaves 3, one of which is BB: $\mathbf{\frac13}$.
(b) Each child is one of 14 equally likely (sex, day) types, so $14^2=196$ families. Families with at least one Tuesday-boy: $196-13^2=27$. Among them, both boys: $7^2-6^2=13$. Answer $\mathbf{\frac{13}{27}}$ -- close to $\frac12$, because the extra detail almost identifies \emph{which} child we're talking about.
\end{example}

\begin{trap}
The answer depends on \emph{how the information was obtained}. ``The older child is a boy'' gives $\frac12$; ``at least one is a boy'' gives $\frac13$. In interviews, state your assumption about how you learned the fact.
\end{trap}

\begin{exercises}
\item A family has two children and the older one is a girl. Probability both are girls? What if you only know at least one is a girl?
\item Of three coins, one is double-headed and two are fair. A coin is picked at random and tossed; it shows heads. Probability it is the double-headed coin?
\item Machines A, B, C produce 50\%, 30\% and 20\% of a factory's output, with defect rates 1\%, 2\% and 3\%. A randomly chosen item is defective. Probability it came from C?
\item Monty Hall with 4 doors: you pick one, the host (who knows) opens one goat door, and you switch to one of the two other closed doors at random. Probability of winning? Compare with staying.
\item Monty Hall where the host does \emph{not} know where the car is, opens one of the other two doors at random, and it happens to show a goat. Probability of winning if you switch?
\item A test is 95\% sensitive (catches 95\% of true cases) and 90\% specific (10\% false positives). Prevalence is 2\%. Probability of disease given a positive test?
\item A coin has an unknown bias $p$, uniformly distributed on $[0,1]$. It lands heads in all of 3 tosses. Probability that the next toss is heads? (Laplace's rule of succession: $\frac{h+1}{n+2}$.)
\end{exercises}

%=====================================================================
\chapter{Counting-Based Probability}\label{ch:cprob}

\section{Cards, dice, birthdays}
\textbf{Deck facts:} 52 cards, 4 suits of 13, 12 face cards. $\C{52}5=2{,}598{,}960$ five-card hands.

\begin{example}[the birthday problem]
How many people do you need for a better than even chance that two share a birthday (365 equally likely days)?

\textbf{Solution.} $P(\text{all distinct among }n)=\frac{365}{365}\cdot\frac{364}{365}\cdots\frac{365-n+1}{365}$. At $n=23$ this drops to $0.493$, so the chance of a match is $\mathbf{50.7\%}$.
\textbf{Intuition:} with 23 people there are $\C{23}2=253$ \emph{pairs}, each matching with probability $\frac1{365}$. Rough estimate: $P(\text{no match})\approx e^{-253/365}\approx0.50$. With 70 people it's 99.9\%.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[poker hands]
Probability of a full house (three of one rank, two of another)?

\textbf{Solution.} Choose the rank of the triple (13) and its suits ($\C43$), then the pair's rank (12) and suits ($\C42$):
$13\cdot4\cdot12\cdot6=3744$. $P=\frac{3744}{2598960}\approx\mathbf{0.144\%}$.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[de M\'er\'e's paradox]
Which is more likely: at least one six in 4 rolls of a die, or at least one double-six in 24 rolls of two dice?

\textbf{Solution.} $1-\left(\frac56\right)^4=0.518$ vs $1-\left(\frac{35}{36}\right)^{24}=0.491$. The first. (Gamblers in the 1650s noticed this from experience; the puzzle helped launch probability theory.)
\end{example}

\section{The hypergeometric pattern}
Drawing $n$ items without replacement from $N$, of which $K$ are ``special'':
\[P(\text{exactly }k\text{ special})=\frac{\C Kk\C{N-K}{n-k}}{\C Nn}.\]

\begin{exercises}
\item What is the probability that a 5-card hand contains exactly 2 aces?
\item What is the probability that a 5-card hand is a flush (all one suit) but not a straight flush? (There are 40 straight flushes, including royal flushes.)
\item (Newton--Pepys) Which is most likely: at least one six with 6 dice, at least two sixes with 12 dice, or at least three sixes with 18 dice?
\item A committee of 4 is chosen at random from 6 men and 4 women. Probability it has at least 2 women?
\item 8 people sit randomly around a round table. Probability that A and B sit together?
\item The letters of BANANA are arranged at random. Probability that the two N's are not adjacent?
\end{exercises}

%=====================================================================
\chapter{Distributions Toolkit}\label{ch:distr}

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{llll}
\toprule
Distribution & Story & $P(X=k)$ & Mean, variance\\\midrule
Bernoulli$(p)$ & one trial & $p$ or $1-p$ & $p$, $p(1-p)$\\
Binomial$(n,p)$ & successes in $n$ independent trials & $\C nk p^k(1-p)^{n-k}$ & $np$, $np(1-p)$\\
Geometric$(p)$ & trials until first success & $(1-p)^{k-1}p$ & $\frac1p$, $\frac{1-p}{p^2}$\\
Hypergeometric & successes without replacement & see Ch.~\ref{ch:cprob} & $n\frac KN$\\
Poisson$(\lambda)$ & count of rare events in an interval & $e^{-\lambda}\frac{\lambda^k}{k!}$ & $\lambda$, $\lambda$\\
Uniform$(a,b)$ & continuous, flat & density $\frac1{b-a}$ & $\frac{a+b}2$, $\frac{(b-a)^2}{12}$\\
Exponential$(\lambda)$ & waiting time between Poisson events & density $\lambda e^{-\lambda x}$ & $\frac1\lambda$, $\frac1{\lambda^2}$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\begin{why}[Why the geometric mean is $1/p$]
Let $E$ be the expected number of trials. The first trial succeeds with probability $p$ (done in 1); otherwise you've used 1 trial and are back at the start: $E=1+(1-p)E\Rightarrow E=\frac1p$. This ``first-step'' argument is the engine of Chapter~\ref{ch:fsa}.
\end{why}

\textbf{Memorylessness.} Geometric and exponential waiting times ``forget'' the past: having waited 10 minutes for a Poisson-process bus, your expected remaining wait is unchanged. Real buses aren't Poisson -- that's the inspection paradox (Chapter~\ref{ch:geo}).

\begin{example}[real life: call centre]
A support desk gets 3 calls per hour on average (Poisson). Probability of no calls in an hour? At least 2?

\textbf{Solution.} $P(0)=e^{-3}\approx\mathbf{0.050}$. $P(\ge2)=1-e^{-3}(1+3)\approx\mathbf{0.801}$.
\end{example}

\begin{exercises}
\item A family has 4 children (boys and girls equally likely). Probability of exactly 2 boys?
\item A student guesses all 10 questions of an MCQ test, each with 4 options. Probability of at least 8 correct? Expected number correct?
\item Probability that the first six appears on the third roll of a die?
\item Using the call-centre numbers, what is the probability of exactly 3 calls in an hour?
\item Expected number of rolls to get a six, and the variance of that number?
\end{exercises}

%=====================================================================
\chapter{Expectation and the Linearity Trick}\label{ch:exp}

\[\E[X]=\sum_x x\,P(X=x),\qquad \E[X+Y]=\E[X]+\E[Y]\ \ \textbf{always, even if $X,Y$ are dependent}.\]

\begin{idea}[Indicator variables]
To find the expected \emph{number} of things that happen, write $X=I_1+I_2+\dots+I_n$, where $I_j=1$ if thing $j$ happens. Then
\[\E[X]=P(\text{thing 1})+P(\text{thing 2})+\cdots+P(\text{thing }n).\]
You never need the distribution of $X$. This turns many ``impossible'' puzzles into one-liners.
\end{idea}

\begin{example}[hat-check]
$n$ people's hats are returned at random. Expected number of people who get their own hat?

\textbf{Solution.} Each person gets their own hat with probability $\frac1n$. Sum over $n$ people: $\mathbf1$, for every $n$. (Compare with derangements: the \emph{probability} of zero matches is about $\frac1e$.)
\end{example}

\begin{example}[distinct faces]
A die is rolled 6 times. Expected number of distinct faces seen?

\textbf{Solution.} Face $j$ is seen with probability $1-\left(\frac56\right)^6$. Six faces: $6\left(1-\left(\frac56\right)^6\right)\approx\mathbf{3.99}$.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[coupon collector]
Expected number of rolls to see all six faces?

\textbf{Solution.} After seeing $k$ faces, each roll shows a new face with probability $\frac{6-k}6$, so the wait is geometric with mean $\frac6{6-k}$. Add the stages:
\[6\left(\frac16+\frac15+\frac14+\frac13+\frac12+1\right)=\mathbf{14.7}.\]
In general $nH_n\approx n\ln n$. (Real life: how many packs of cricket cards until you complete the set.)
\end{example}

\begin{example}[maximum of two dice]
Expected value of the larger of two dice?

\textbf{Solution.} $P(\max\le k)=\left(\frac k6\right)^2$, so $P(\max=k)=\frac{k^2-(k-1)^2}{36}=\frac{2k-1}{36}$.
$\E=\frac{1\cdot1+2\cdot3+3\cdot5+4\cdot7+5\cdot9+6\cdot11}{36}=\frac{161}{36}\approx\mathbf{4.47}$.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[couples at a round table]
Four couples sit at random around a round table. Expected number of couples sitting together?

\textbf{Solution.} A given person's partner is one of 7 others, 2 of whom are neighbours: probability $\frac27$. Four couples: $\frac87\approx\mathbf{1.14}$.
\end{example}

\begin{shortcut}[Speed shortcut: the tail-sum formula]
For a non-negative integer variable, $\E[X]=\sum_{k\ge1}P(X\ge k)$. For a non-negative continuous variable, $\E[X]=\int_0^\infty P(X>x)\,dx$.
\end{shortcut}

\begin{exercises}
\item Expected value of the smaller of two dice?
\item 10 balls are thrown at random into 5 boxes. Expected number of empty boxes?
\item Cards are drawn one by one without replacement from a shuffled deck. Expected number of cards drawn up to and including the first ace? (Hint: the 4 aces split the 48 other cards into 5 gaps, each of expected size $\frac{48}5$.)
\item Expected number of runs (maximal blocks of identical outcomes) in 10 fair coin tosses?
\item A die is rolled until a six appears. Expected sum of all the rolls, including the six?
\item Among 30 people, expected number of pairs who share a birthday?
\end{exercises}

%=====================================================================
\chapter{First-Step Analysis and Markov Chains}\label{ch:fsa}

\section{The method}
\begin{idea}[First-step analysis]
Define a \textbf{state} that captures everything relevant about the past. Let $E_s$ be the expected time (or $p_s$ the probability of winning) from state $s$. Condition on the \emph{first step}:
\[E_s=1+\sum_{t}P(s\to t)\,E_t,\qquad p_s=\sum_tP(s\to t)\,p_t.\]
Solve the linear equations.
\end{idea}

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[>={Latex},font=\scriptsize]
\node[circle,draw,minimum size=9mm] (0) at (0,0){$S_0$}; \node[circle,draw,minimum size=9mm] (1) at (3.2,0){$S_1$}; \node[circle,draw,double,minimum size=9mm] (2) at (6.4,0){HH};
\draw[->] (0)--node[above]{H, $\frac12$}(1); \draw[->] (1)--node[above]{H, $\frac12$}(2);
\draw[->] (1) to[bend left=35] node[below]{T, $\frac12$} (0); \draw[->] (0) to[loop left] node[left]{T, $\frac12$} (0);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}
A \textbf{state diagram} makes the equations automatic: there is one equation per state and one term per outgoing arrow.
\begin{example}[waiting for HH]
Expected number of fair-coin tosses until two heads in a row?

\textbf{Solution.} States: $S_0$ (no progress), $S_1$ (last toss H).
\[E_0=1+\tfrac12E_1+\tfrac12E_0,\qquad E_1=1+\tfrac12\cdot0+\tfrac12E_0.\]
From the first, $E_0=2+E_1$; substituting, $E_1=1+\frac12(2+E_1)\Rightarrow E_1=4$, $E_0=\mathbf6$.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[HT is faster than HH]
Expected tosses until HT?

\textbf{Solution.} Wait for the first H (expected 2), then wait for a T (expected 2): \textbf{4}. Unlike HH, a failure (another H) doesn't destroy progress -- you're still one step away.
\end{example}

\begin{shortcut}[Speed shortcut: Conway's formula for pattern waiting times]
For a fair coin, the expected wait for a pattern is $\sum 2^k$ over all $k$ such that the first $k$ symbols of the pattern equal its last $k$ symbols (always including $k=$ full length). HH: $2+4=6$. HT: $4$. HHH: $2+4+8=14$. HTH: $2+8=10$. With a die, replace 2 by 6: two consecutive sixes take $6+36=42$ rolls.
\end{shortcut}

\section{Races between patterns (Penney's game)}
\begin{example}
A fair coin is tossed until either HH or TH appears. Probability HH comes first?

\textbf{Solution.} If the first two tosses are HH (probability $\frac14$), HH wins. Otherwise a T appears before any HH, and from then on the first HH must be preceded by a T, so TH appears first. $P=\mathbf{\frac14}$.
\end{example}

\textbf{Penney's game:} for any pattern of length 3 your opponent picks, there is a pattern that beats it. If they pick HHT, you pick THH and win with probability $\frac34$. Patterns are \emph{non-transitive}, like rock--paper--scissors.

\section{Gambler's ruin}
You have \Rs$i$; each round you win \Rs1 with probability $p$ or lose \Rs1 with probability $q=1-p$. You stop at \Rs0 or \Rs$N$.
\[P(\text{reach }N)=\begin{cases}\dfrac iN & p=\frac12\\[8pt] \dfrac{1-(q/p)^i}{1-(q/p)^N} & p\neq\frac12\end{cases}\qquad\E[\text{duration}]=i(N-i)\ \text{ when }p=\tfrac12.\]
\begin{why}
For $p=\frac12$, first-step analysis gives $p_i=\frac12p_{i+1}+\frac12p_{i-1}$, so the $p_i$ lie on a straight line from $p_0=0$ to $p_N=1$. A fair game keeps your expected wealth constant: $i=N\cdot P(\text{reach }N)$.
\end{why}

\textit{Real life:} This is why a small edge for the house is fatal over many rounds: at $p=0.4$, starting with \Rs5 and aiming for \Rs10, you reach your target only about 12\% of the time.

\begin{example}[ant on a cube]
An ant walks along the edges of a cube, choosing one of the 3 edges at random at each vertex. Expected number of moves from one vertex to the opposite vertex?

\textbf{Solution.} Use distance from the target as the state: $E_3=1+E_2$; $E_2=1+\frac23E_1+\frac13E_3$; $E_1=1+\frac13\cdot0+\frac23E_2$. Solving: $E_1=7$, $E_2=9$, $E_3=\mathbf{10}$.
\end{example}

\begin{exercises}
\item Expected number of fair-coin tosses until HHH?
\item Expected number of die rolls until two consecutive sixes?
\item Expected tosses until HTH? Until HHT?
\item You start with \Rs5 and bet \Rs1 per round on a fair coin, stopping at \Rs0 or \Rs10. Probability of reaching \Rs10? Expected number of rounds?
\item Same as above, but you win each round with probability 0.4. Probability of reaching \Rs10?
\item A random walk starts at 0 and moves $\pm1$ with equal probability. Probability it hits $+3$ before $-2$?
\item Your opponent chooses HHT in Penney's game. Which pattern should you choose, and what is your probability of winning?
\end{exercises}

%=====================================================================
\chapter{Continuous and Geometric Probability}\label{ch:geo}

When outcomes are points chosen uniformly in a region, \textbf{probability $=$ favourable area (or length) $\div$ total area}.

\begin{example}[the meeting problem]
Two friends agree to meet between 5 and 6 pm. Each arrives at a uniformly random time and waits 15 minutes, then leaves. Probability they meet?

\textbf{Solution.} Plot arrival times $(x,y)$ in a $60\times60$ square. They meet if $|x-y|\le15$. The ``no meeting'' region is two corner triangles, each with legs 45: total area $45^2$.
\[P=1-\frac{45^2}{60^2}=1-\frac9{16}=\mathbf{\frac7{16}}.\]
\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.045]
\fill[blue!15] (0,0)--(15,0)--(60,45)--(60,60)--(45,60)--(0,15)--cycle;
\draw (0,0) rectangle (60,60);
\draw (15,0)--(60,45); \draw (0,15)--(45,60);
\node at (30,30) {\small meet}; \node at (48,12) {\scriptsize no}; \node at (12,48) {\scriptsize no};
\node[below] at (30,0) {\scriptsize $x$}; \node[left] at (0,30) {\scriptsize $y$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}
\end{example}

\begin{example}[broken stick]
A stick is broken at two uniformly random points. Probability the three pieces form a triangle?

\textbf{Solution.} A triangle needs every piece shorter than $\frac12$. With break points $(x,y)$ in the unit square, the valid region consists of two triangles of total area $\frac14$. $P=\mathbf{\frac14}$.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[points on a circle]
Three points are chosen uniformly on a circle. Probability they all lie in some semicircle?

\textbf{Solution.} For each point, the event ``the other two lie within the semicircle starting at this point and going clockwise'' has probability $\left(\frac12\right)^2$. These three events are disjoint (at most one point can be the ``first'' of a semicircle containing all). Total $3\times\frac14=\mathbf{\frac34}$. In general, $n$ points: $\frac n{2^{n-1}}$.
(The triangle they form is acute exactly when they are \emph{not} in a semicircle: probability $1/4$.)
\end{example}

\textbf{Order statistics of uniforms:} for $n$ independent Uniform$(0,1)$ variables, $\E[\max]=\frac n{n+1}$, $\E[\min]=\frac1{n+1}$. The $n$ points split $[0,1]$ into $n+1$ pieces of equal expected length.

\begin{trap}[Bertrand's paradox]
``A random chord of a circle'' has no single meaning: choosing random endpoints, a random midpoint, or a random distance from the centre give different answers ($\frac13$, $\frac14$, $\frac12$ for ``longer than the side of the inscribed triangle''). In an interview, define your randomness before computing.
\end{trap}

\begin{exercises}
\item Two friends arrive uniformly at random between 5 and 6 pm and each waits 20 minutes. Probability they meet?
\item A stick of length 1 is broken at a uniformly random point. Expected length of the longer piece?
\item Expected value of the maximum of three independent Uniform$(0,1)$ variables?
\item $X,Y$ are independent Uniform$(0,1)$. Find $P(X+Y<1)$ and $P(X^2+Y^2<1)$.
\item Four points are chosen uniformly on a circle. Probability they all lie in some semicircle?
\item A point is chosen uniformly in a unit square. Probability that it lies within 0.1 of the nearest side?
\item (Inspection paradox) Buses come exactly every 10 minutes and you arrive at a random time: expected wait? If instead buses arrive as a Poisson process averaging one every 10 minutes: expected wait?
\end{exercises}

%=====================================================================
\chapter{Optimal Stopping, Games and Betting}\label{ch:stop}

\section{Backward induction}
\begin{example}[the reroll game]
Roll a die and receive its face value in rupees -- but you may reroll once (and must accept the second roll). What's the game worth, and when should you reroll?

\textbf{Solution.} Work backwards. The final roll is worth 3.5. On the first roll, keep it if it beats 3.5, i.e.\ keep 4, 5, 6; reroll 1, 2, 3.
\[V_2=\frac{4+5+6}6+\frac36\times3.5=2.5+1.75=\mathbf{4.25}.\]
With up to three rolls, keep the first roll only if it beats 4.25 (5 or 6): $V_3=\frac{11}6+\frac46\times4.25=\frac{14}3\approx4.67$.
\end{example}

\begin{why}
At each decision, compare what you have with the \emph{expected value of continuing optimally}. That continuation value is computed from the end first, hence ``backward'' induction. It's the same logic as valuing an American option on a binomial tree: at each node, take the maximum of exercising now and the discounted value of continuing.
\end{why}

\section{The secretary problem}
$n$ candidates arrive in random order; you see each one's relative rank and must accept or reject on the spot. To maximise the chance of picking the very best: \textbf{reject the first $n/e\approx37\%$, then take the first candidate better than all of them}. Success probability $\to\frac1e\approx\mathbf{37\%}$.

\section{Betting: expectation is not everything}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{St Petersburg paradox:} a coin is tossed until the first head; if that takes $k$ tosses you win \Rs$2^k$. The expected payout is $\sum_k\frac1{2^k}\cdot2^k=\infty$, yet nobody would pay much to play. Resolution: utility (diminishing value of money) and finite bankrolls.
\item \textbf{Martingale (doubling) strategy:} doubling after every loss ``guarantees'' a \Rs1 profit, but a finite bankroll means a rare, huge loss exactly cancels the frequent small wins. Expected profit stays 0 in a fair game and is negative in an unfair one.
\item \textbf{Kelly criterion:} for a bet paying $b$-to-1 that you win with probability $p$, the fraction of wealth that maximises long-run growth is
\[f^*=\frac{bp-q}{b}=p-\frac qb.\]
Bet nothing if $f^*\le0$. Many traders use a fraction (like half-Kelly) because the true $p$ is uncertain.
\end{itemize}

\begin{example}[Russian roulette decision]
A 6-chamber revolver has 2 bullets in \emph{adjacent} chambers. The cylinder is spun, the trigger pulled on you, and it clicks (empty). It's your turn again: is it better to pull again immediately or to spin first?

\textbf{Solution.} Spinning: survival $\frac46=\frac23$. Not spinning: the 4 empty chambers form a block; the one just fired is one of these 4, and only the last of the block is followed by a bullet. Survival $\frac34$. \textbf{Don't spin.}
\end{example}

\begin{trap}[The two-envelope paradox]
One envelope has twice the money of the other. You hold one with amount $x$. ``The other has $2x$ or $x/2$ with equal probability, so its expected value is $1.25x$; always switch'' -- and then switch back, forever. The flaw: $x$ is not fixed while the ``other'' varies; the equal-probability assumption can't hold for every $x$ under any proper prior. Switching gains nothing on average.
\end{trap}

\begin{exercises}
\item In the reroll game with up to 3 rolls, state the full strategy and check the value $\frac{14}3$.
\item You may roll a die repeatedly, paying \Rs1 per roll (including the first), and you may stop at any time and receive the face value of your last roll. What is the game worth, and when should you stop?
\item Kelly fractions: (a) an even-money bet you win with probability 0.55; (b) a bet paying 2-to-1 that you win with probability 0.4.
\item With 100 candidates in the secretary problem, roughly how many should you reject outright, and what is your chance of picking the best?
\item A martingale gambler has \Rs1023, starts with a \Rs1 bet on a fair coin, and doubles after each loss. How many consecutive losses can he cover, and what is the probability of going broke in one ``cycle''? What is his expected profit per cycle?
\end{exercises}

%=====================================================================
\chapter{Variance, Covariance and Correlation}\label{ch:var}

Expectation tells you the centre, and variance tells you how far you stray from it. Quant and risk interviews care at least as much about the second.

\section{Definitions}
\[\text{Var}(X)=\E[(X-\E X)^2]=\E[X^2]-(\E X)^2,\]
\[\text{Cov}(X,Y)=\E[XY]-\E[X]\E[Y],\qquad\rho=\frac{\text{Cov}(X,Y)}{\sigma_X\sigma_Y}.\]

\begin{idea}[The rules]
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
\item $\text{Var}(aX+b)=a^2\text{Var}(X)$. Constants do not add spread, and scaling squares.
\item $\text{Var}(X+Y)=\text{Var}(X)+\text{Var}(Y)+2\text{Cov}(X,Y)$.
\item Independent $\Rightarrow$ Cov $=0$, so variances add. \textbf{Standard deviations never simply add}, except when $\rho=1$.
\item For the mean of $n$ independent copies: $\text{Var}(\bar X)=\frac{\sigma^2}n$, so $\sigma_{\bar X}=\frac\sigma{\sqrt n}$.
\item $\text{Cov}(aX+bY,\ cU+dV)$ expands like a product of brackets.
\item $-1\le\rho\le1$, with $\pm1$ exactly when $Y$ is a linear function of $X$.
\end{itemize}
\end{idea}

\begin{why}[Why the $\sqrt n$ matters]
Add $n$ independent bets. Their total's mean grows like $n$, but its standard deviation only grows like $\sqrt n$, since variances add and not standard deviations. Relative risk shrinks like $\frac1{\sqrt n}$. This single fact underlies casinos, insurance and diversification. It is also why reducing a poll's error by half takes 4 times the sample.
\end{why}

\begin{trap}
Zero covariance does not mean independent. Take $X$ uniform on $\{-1,0,1\}$ and $Y=X^2$. $\E[XY]=\E[X^3]=0$ and $\E[X]=0$, so Cov $=0$. But $Y$ is completely determined by $X$.
\end{trap}

\section{Variance by indicators}
For $X=\sum I_j$:
\[\text{Var}(X)=\sum_j\text{Var}(I_j)+\sum_{i\ne j}\text{Cov}(I_i,I_j),\quad\text{Var}(I_j)=p_j(1-p_j).\]
\begin{example}[hat-check variance]
$n$ hats returned at random: find the variance of the number of matches.

\textbf{Solution.} $\text{Var}(I_j)=\frac1n\left(1-\frac1n\right)$. For $i\ne j$: $\E[I_iI_j]=\frac1{n(n-1)}$, so $\text{Cov}=\frac1{n(n-1)}-\frac1{n^2}=\frac1{n^2(n-1)}$. There are $n(n-1)$ ordered pairs. Total $=\left(1-\frac1n\right)+\frac1n=\mathbf{1}$, for every $n\ge2$. Both mean and variance are 1, just like a Poisson(1), which is indeed the limit.
\end{example}

\section{Two-asset portfolios}
\begin{example}[real life: diversification]
Assets A and B have volatilities 20\% and 30\%, with correlation 0.5. Find the volatility of a 50/50 portfolio.

\textbf{Solution.} $\sigma_p^2=0.25(0.04)+0.25(0.09)+2(0.25)(0.5)(0.2)(0.3)=0.01+0.0225+0.015=0.0475$. So $\sigma_p\approx\mathbf{21.8\%}$. That beats the 25\% that plain averaging would suggest, and the gap is the benefit of diversification. It disappears only when $\rho=1$.
\end{example}
\textbf{Minimum-variance weight in A:} $w^*=\dfrac{\sigma_B^2-\rho\sigma_A\sigma_B}{\sigma_A^2+\sigma_B^2-2\rho\sigma_A\sigma_B}$. (Differentiate the variance in $w$, and set it to 0.)

\begin{exercises}
\item Find the variance of one die roll, and of the sum of two independent dice.
\item $X\sim\text{Binomial}(100,0.3)$. Find its mean, variance and standard deviation.
\item Var$(X)=4$, Var$(Y)=1$ and Cov$(X,Y)=1$. Find Var$(2X-3Y+5)$.
\item $X$ and $Y$ are independent with the same variance. Find the correlation of $X$ and $X+Y$.
\item Using the data of the portfolio example, find the minimum-variance weight in A.
\item 100 independent measurements each have SD 15. Find the SD of their mean.
\item Two dice are rolled. Find Cov(max, min). (Hint: max $\times$ min $=$ the product of the dice.)
\item $X$ has mean 2 and variance 5. Find $\E[X^2]$.
\item A portfolio holds $n$ independent assets, each with volatility 30\%, in equal weights. How large must $n$ be for the portfolio volatility to fall to 10\%?
\item Toss a fair coin 10 times; let $X$ be heads and $Y$ tails. Find the correlation of $X$ and $Y$.
\end{exercises}

%=====================================================================
\chapter{The Normal Distribution, LLN and CLT}\label{ch:clt}

\section{The bell curve}
\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}
\begin{axis}[width=10.5cm,height=4.6cm,axis lines=left,xmin=-3.6,xmax=3.6,ymin=0,ymax=0.46,ytick=\empty,xtick={-3,-2,-1,0,1,2,3},xticklabels={$\mu{-}3\sigma$,$\mu{-}2\sigma$,$\mu{-}\sigma$,$\mu$,$\mu{+}\sigma$,$\mu{+}2\sigma$,$\mu{+}3\sigma$},tick label style={font=\scriptsize}]
\addplot[fill=blue!8,draw=none,domain=-3:3,samples=60]{exp(-x*x/2)/sqrt(2*pi)}\closedcycle;
\addplot[fill=blue!18,draw=none,domain=-2:2,samples=60]{exp(-x*x/2)/sqrt(2*pi)}\closedcycle;
\addplot[fill=blue!32,draw=none,domain=-1:1,samples=60]{exp(-x*x/2)/sqrt(2*pi)}\closedcycle;
\addplot[blue!60!black,thick,domain=-3.6:3.6,samples=100]{exp(-x*x/2)/sqrt(2*pi)};
\node[font=\scriptsize] at (axis cs:0,0.12){68\%};
\node[font=\scriptsize] at (axis cs:1.5,0.045){95\%};
\node[font=\scriptsize] at (axis cs:2.55,0.035){99.7\%};
\end{axis}
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}
\textbf{Standardise:} if $X\sim N(\mu,\sigma^2)$, then $Z=\frac{X-\mu}\sigma\sim N(0,1)$.

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{c|ccccccccc}
\toprule
$z$ & 0.5 & 1.0 & 1.28 & 1.5 & 1.645 & 1.96 & 2.0 & 2.33 & 3.0\\\midrule
$P(Z>z)$ & 30.9\% & 15.9\% & 10\% & 6.7\% & 5\% & 2.5\% & 2.3\% & 1\% & 0.13\%\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\textbf{Sums:} independent normals add to a normal, with means and variances added.

\section{Law of large numbers}
The average of $n$ independent copies of $X$ converges to $\E[X]$. That is what makes expected value practical: in the long run, your average per-play result equals the expectation. It does \emph{not} say that a losing streak is ``due'' to reverse. Later plays just swamp the early ones.

\begin{trap}[Common trap: gambler's fallacy]
After 10 reds, the next spin of a fair wheel is still 50--50. The law of large numbers works by dilution, not compensation. The excess of reds stays about the same size while the total count grows.
\end{trap}

\section{Central limit theorem}
\begin{idea}[CLT]
Add up many independent, identically distributed variables with mean $\mu$ and variance $\sigma^2$. Then, whatever their original shape,
\[S_n\approx N(n\mu,\ n\sigma^2),\qquad\bar X\approx N\left(\mu,\ \frac{\sigma^2}n\right).\]
\end{idea}
\textbf{Normal approximation to binomial:} $\text{Bin}(n,p)\approx N(np,\ np(1-p))$, good when $np$ and $n(1-p)$ are both at least about 10. For discrete counts, use the \textbf{continuity correction}: $P(X\ge60)\approx P(Y\ge59.5)$.

\begin{example}[coin]
In 100 fair tosses, estimate the chance of at least 60 heads.

\textbf{Solution.} Mean 50, SD 5. $z=\frac{59.5-50}5=1.9$. $P\approx\mathbf{2.9\%}$. (The exact value is 2.84\%.)
\end{example}

\begin{example}[real life: scaling risk]
A stock has a daily volatility of 1\%. Estimate its annual volatility, with 252 trading days and independent days.

\textbf{Solution.} $1\%\times\sqrt{252}\approx\mathbf{15.9\%}$. Variances add over time, so volatility scales with $\sqrt{\text{time}}$.
\end{example}

\section{Confidence intervals and testing}
\[\text{95\% confidence interval for a mean: }\bar x\pm1.96\frac s{\sqrt n};\qquad\text{for a proportion: }\hat p\pm1.96\sqrt{\frac{\hat p(1-\hat p)}n}.\]
\textbf{Sample size} for margin $m$ on a proportion (worst case $p=\frac12$): $n\approx\frac{1.96^2\cdot0.25}{m^2}\approx\frac1{m^2}$.

\begin{example}[poll]
How many people must be polled for a $\pm3\%$ margin at 95\% confidence?

\textbf{Solution.} $\frac{0.9604}{0.0009}\approx\mathbf{1067}$. This is why national polls so often use about 1,000 people, however large the country.
\end{example}

\textbf{Hypothesis test:} assume the boring explanation (the null), and calculate how surprising the data would be. A \textbf{p-value} is the probability of data at least this extreme \emph{if the null is true}. It is \emph{not} the probability that the null is true. Getting those two confused is a Bayes error (Chapter~\ref{ch:bayes}).

\begin{exercises}
\item IQ scores are $N(100,15^2)$. What fraction exceed 130? What fraction lie between 85 and 115?
\item Marks have mean 60 and SD 8. Roughly what score marks the top 16\%?
\item In 400 fair tosses, estimate the chance of between 190 and 210 heads inclusive, with the continuity correction.
\item 100 dice are rolled. Estimate the chance that the total exceeds 380.
\item A survey wants its error cut in half. By what factor must the sample grow?
\item In a poll of 400, 55\% favour a proposal. Give the 95\% confidence interval.
\item $X\sim N(10,9)$ and $Y\sim N(20,16)$ are independent. Find $P(X+Y>35)$ and $P(X>Y)$.
\item Daily volatility is 1.5\%. Estimate annual volatility (252 days).
\item A coin gives 60 heads in 100 tosses. Is this significant at the 5\% level (two-sided, no continuity correction)?
\item Height is $N(170,10^2)$. Find the probability that a person exceeds 185. Among 100 people, how many would you expect?
\end{exercises}

%=====================================================================
\chapter{Conditional Expectation and Recursion}\label{ch:tower}

\section{The tower law}
\begin{idea}[Conditional expectation]
Break a hard average into easier conditional averages:
\[\E[X]=\E\big[\E[X\mid Y]\big]=\sum_y\E[X\mid Y=y]\,P(Y=y).\]
\textbf{Total variance:} $\text{Var}(X)=\E[\text{Var}(X\mid Y)]+\text{Var}(\E[X\mid Y])$, or within-group plus between-group.
\end{idea}

\begin{example}[a random number of coin flips]
Roll a die, then flip that many fair coins. Find the mean and variance of the number of heads.

\textbf{Solution.} Given $N$, heads is Bin$(N,\frac12)$: conditional mean $\frac N2$, conditional variance $\frac N4$.
$\E=\frac{3.5}2=\mathbf{1.75}$. Var $=\E\left[\frac N4\right]+\text{Var}\left(\frac N2\right)=\frac{3.5}4+\frac14\cdot\frac{35}{12}\approx0.875+0.729=\mathbf{1.604}$.
\end{example}

\section{Random sums (Wald)}
If $N$ is independent of the i.i.d.\ $X_i$, then for $S=X_1+\dots+X_N$:
\[\E[S]=\E[N]\,\E[X],\qquad\text{Var}(S)=\E[N]\,\text{Var}(X)+\text{Var}(N)\,(\E X)^2.\]
\textit{Real life:} insurance claims. The number of claims and the size of each claim both contribute to the risk.

\section{Who shoots first}
\begin{example}[alternating turns]
A and B alternate tossing a coin (A first). The first to get heads wins. Find A's chance.

\textbf{Solution.} Let $w$ be the probability that whoever goes first wins. $w=\frac12+\frac12(1-w)$, since if the first player misses, they are now effectively second. So $w=\mathbf{\frac23}$.

With general hit rates $a$ and $b$: $P(\text{A wins})=\dfrac{a}{1-(1-a)(1-b)}$. The denominator is the chance that the round ends.
\end{example}

\section{Famous recursive results}
\begin{example}[uniform sums reach 1]
Add Uniform$(0,1)$ numbers until the sum exceeds 1. Find the expected count.

\textbf{Solution.} $P(N>n)=P(U_1+\dots+U_n\le1)=\frac1{n!}$, the volume of a simplex. By the tail-sum formula, $\E[N]=\sum_{n\ge0}\frac1{n!}=\mathbf{e}$.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[P\'olya's urn]
An urn holds 1 red and 1 blue ball. Draw one, and return it with another of the same colour. Repeat. Find the chance that the second draw is red, and describe the long-run share of red.

\textbf{Solution.} $\frac12\cdot\frac23+\frac12\cdot\frac13=\mathbf{\frac12}$. By symmetry, every draw has the same chance of red, though draws are far from independent. Remarkably, the long-run proportion of red is itself \emph{uniform} on $(0,1)$. Early luck gets locked in, which is a toy model of ``rich get richer''.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[first success without replacement]
An urn has $r$ red and $b$ blue balls. Find the expected draws to the first red.

\textbf{Solution.} Each blue ball is drawn before all the reds with probability $\frac1{r+1}$, since among it and the $r$ reds, each is equally likely to be first. So expected blues before the first red $=\frac b{r+1}$. Answer: $1+\frac b{r+1}=\mathbf{\frac{r+b+1}{r+1}}$.
\end{example}

\begin{trap}[Common trap: conditioning changes the distribution]
``Roll a die until a 6 appears. Given that all rolls were even, find the expected number of rolls.'' There are two tempting answers: 6 (the usual wait for a six), or 3 (one six among the three even faces). The truth is $\frac32$. Conditioning on ``no odd rolls'' discards all the long sequences, as those are the most likely to include an odd roll. Then $P(N=n\mid\text{all even})\propto\left(\frac13\right)^{n-1}$, which is geometric with success rate $\frac23$.
\end{trap}

\begin{exercises}
\item Roll a die, then roll that many dice. Find the expected total on those dice.
\item Toss a coin until the first head. Then roll a die that many times. Find the expected sum of the rolls.
\item A and B take alternate shots, A first, with hit rates $\frac13$ and $\frac12$. Find A's chance of hitting first.
\item An urn has 3 red and 7 blue balls. Find the expected draws without replacement to the first red.
\item A P\'olya urn starts with 2 red and 1 blue. Find the chance that the second draw is red.
\item A stick of length 1 is broken at a random point and the left part is kept. This is done twice. Find the expected final length.
\item Customers per day are Poisson(10), spending a mean of \Rs200 with SD \Rs50, independently. Find the mean and SD of daily revenue.
\item Pick $U_1$ uniform on $(0,1)$, then $U_2$ uniform on $(0,U_1)$. Find $\E[U_2]$.
\item Roll a die until a 6 appears. How many 2s do you expect before that 6? How many non-six rolls?
\item A coin is chosen at random from 2 fair coins and 1 double-headed coin, and tossed twice. Find the expected number of heads.
\end{exercises}

%=====================================================================
\chapter{Random Walks and Markov Chains in the Long Run}\label{ch:walk}

\section{Symmetric random walk}
$S_n$ moves $\pm1$ with equal probability.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
\item $\E[S_n]=0$ and Var$(S_n)=n$. So the typical distance from the start grows like $\sqrt n$.
\item $P(S_{2n}=0)=\frac{\C{2n}n}{4^n}\approx\frac1{\sqrt{\pi n}}$.
\item Hits $+a$ before $-b$ with probability $\frac b{a+b}$, after $ab$ expected steps.
\item In one or two dimensions the walk returns to the origin with certainty; in three dimensions it may never come back.
\end{itemize}

\section{The reflection principle}
\begin{idea}[Reflection]
The number of paths from $x$ to $y$ that touch a barrier equals the number of \emph{all} paths from $x$'s mirror image to $y$. Reflect the part of the path before the first touch.
\end{idea}
\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.62]
\draw[-{Latex},gray] (0,0)--(9,0) node[right]{\scriptsize $n$};
\draw[dashed,red!60!black] (0,-1)--(8.6,-1) node[right]{\scriptsize barrier $-1$};
\draw[thick,blue!60!black] (0,1)--(1,0)--(2,-1)--(3,0)--(4,1)--(5,0)--(6,1)--(7,2)--(8,3);
\draw[thick,orange!70!black,dashed] (0,-3)--(1,-2)--(2,-1);
\fill (0,1) circle(2pt) node[left]{\scriptsize start}; \fill (0,-3) circle(2pt) node[left]{\scriptsize mirror}; \fill (8,3) circle(2pt) node[right]{\scriptsize end};
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}
This is the same tool that gave us Catalan numbers (Chapter~\ref{ch:cat}), and it gives the \textbf{ballot theorem}: if A gets $a$ votes and B gets $b<a$, A leads throughout with probability $\frac{a-b}{a+b}$.

\section{Martingales and optional stopping}
A \textbf{martingale} is a fair-game process: the expected next value equals the current value. \textbf{Optional stopping:} under reasonable conditions, $\E[\text{value at a stopping time}]=$ starting value. No stopping rule can turn a fair game into a winning one.
\begin{example}[two lines of proof]
A walk starts at 0 and stops at $+a$ or $-b$. Find the probability of $+a$ and the expected duration.

\textbf{Solution.} $S_n$ is a martingale: $0=a\,p-b(1-p)$, so $p=\frac b{a+b}$. And $S_n^2-n$ is a martingale: $\E[S_T^2]=\E[T]$, so $\E[T]=a^2p+b^2(1-p)=ab$.
\end{example}
\textbf{Biased walk:} with $p>\frac12$, the chance of ever dropping to $-k$ is $\left(\frac qp\right)^k$. That is the risk of ruin for someone with a positive edge.

\section{Stationary distributions}
A Markov chain's next state depends only on the current state. Under mild conditions, it settles into a \textbf{stationary distribution} $\pi$ with $\pi=\pi P$, whatever the start.
\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=1,>={Latex}]
\node[circle,draw,minimum size=9mm] (R) at (0,0){\small Rain}; \node[circle,draw,minimum size=9mm] (S) at (3.6,0){\small Sun};
\draw[->] (R) to[bend left=25] node[above,font=\scriptsize]{0.4} (S); \draw[->] (S) to[bend left=25] node[below,font=\scriptsize]{0.2} (R);
\draw[->] (R) to[loop left] node[left,font=\scriptsize]{0.6} (R); \draw[->] (S) to[loop right] node[right,font=\scriptsize]{0.8} (S);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}
\begin{example}[weather]
Using the chain above, find the long-run fraction of rainy days.

\textbf{Solution.} Flows balance: $\pi_R\times0.4=\pi_S\times0.2$. So $\pi_R:\pi_S=1:2$ and $\pi_R=\mathbf{\frac13}$. A rainy day recurs on average every $\frac1{\pi_R}=3$ days: the expected return time to a state is $\frac1{\pi}$.
\end{example}
\textbf{Random walk on a graph:} $\pi$ at each vertex is proportional to its degree. On a regular graph, such as a cycle or a cube, the walk is uniform, so the expected return time equals the number of vertices. PageRank is a stationary distribution on the graph of web links.

\textbf{Hitting times on a cycle of $n$ nodes:} from distance $k$, the expected time to reach the target is $k(n-k)$. This is gambler's ruin in disguise.

\begin{exercises}
\item Find the stationary distribution of the chain with $P(A\to A)=0.9$, $P(A\to B)=0.1$, $P(B\to A)=0.5$, $P(B\to B)=0.5$.
\item A symmetric walk takes 6 steps. Find the probability that it ends where it began.
\item Among 6-step walks from 0 ending at $+2$, how many touch $-1$ along the way?
\item A symmetric walk starts at 0 and stops at $+4$ or $-6$. Find the probability of $+4$, and the expected duration.
\item A walk has $p=0.6$ up and $q=0.4$ down. Find the probability of ever reaching $-1$, and of ever reaching $-3$.
\item You start with \Rs10 in a fair game, stopping at \Rs0 or \Rs30. Find your chance of reaching \Rs30.
\item A fly walks randomly on the 8 vertices of a cube. Find the expected time to return to its starting vertex.
\item A walker on a cycle of 8 nodes starts opposite the target. Find the expected hitting time.
\item In an election A gets 5 votes and B gets 3. Find the chance that A leads throughout the count.
\item A symmetric walk stops on first reaching $+5$ or $-5$. Find the expected number of steps.
\end{exercises}

%=====================================================================
\chapter{Trading, Options and Pricing Questions}\label{ch:trade}

Trading firms ask quick probability questions in a market setting. The underlying idea is usually a single one: \textbf{price equals expected value under the right probabilities}.

\section{Expected value games and market making}
\begin{idea}[How to quote a market]
Asked to ``make a market'' on an unknown quantity (the sum of 3 dice, the number of cars in a city):
\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
\item Estimate the fair value (the mean).
\item Quote a bid below it and an ask above it. The width reflects your uncertainty.
\item If someone trades with you, \textbf{update}: they may know something, so move your quote their way. This is adverse selection.
\end{enumerate}
\end{idea}
\begin{example}[sum of three dice]
Make a market on the sum of 3 dice.

\textbf{Solution.} Mean $=10.5$, and SD $=\sqrt{3\cdot\frac{35}{12}}\approx2.96$. A sensible tight quote is $10$--$11$. If you sell at 11 and the counterparty has seen one die, you can guess it was high. If they buy again at 12, assume it was a 5 or 6.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[convex payoffs]
A die pays you the square of its face. What is it worth? Is that more or less than the square of the average face?

\textbf{Solution.} $\frac{1+4+9+16+25+36}6=\frac{91}6\approx\mathbf{15.17}$, which is more than $3.5^2=12.25$. For a convex payoff $f$, Jensen's inequality gives $\E[f(X)]\ge f(\E[X])$. The gap is why options have value.
\end{example}

\section{Options}
\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}
\begin{axis}[width=5.8cm,height=4.2cm,axis lines=middle,xmin=60,xmax=140,ymin=-15,ymax=45,xtick={100},ytick={0},xlabel={\scriptsize $S_T$},ylabel={\scriptsize payoff},tick label style={font=\scriptsize},title={\small Long call},title style={yshift=-3pt}]
\addplot[blue!60!black,thick] coordinates {(60,0)(100,0)(140,40)};
\end{axis}
\end{tikzpicture}\quad
\begin{tikzpicture}
\begin{axis}[width=5.8cm,height=4.2cm,axis lines=middle,xmin=60,xmax=140,ymin=-15,ymax=45,xtick={100},ytick={0},xlabel={\scriptsize $S_T$},ylabel={\scriptsize payoff},tick label style={font=\scriptsize},title={\small Long put},title style={yshift=-3pt}]
\addplot[red!60!black,thick] coordinates {(60,40)(100,0)(140,0)};
\end{axis}
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}
\textbf{Call:} right to buy at strike $K$, with payoff $\max(S_T-K,0)$. \textbf{Put:} right to sell at $K$, with payoff $\max(K-S_T,0)$.

\textbf{Put--call parity} (European, no dividends): holding a call and shorting a put has payoff $S_T-K$ in every case, the same as a forward. So:
\[C-P=S_0-Ke^{-rT}.\]
\textbf{Bounds and intuition:} options gain value with higher volatility, since the payoff is one-sided. A call's price lies between $\max(S_0-Ke^{-rT},0)$ and $S_0$.

\section{One-step binomial pricing}
\begin{idea}[Replication, not prediction]
The stock is at $S_0$, and next period is $uS_0$ or $dS_0$. Gross riskless return $R$. Then the option's price is its discounted expectation under the \textbf{risk-neutral probability}:
\[q=\frac{R-d}{u-d},\qquad\text{Price}=\frac{q\,V_{\text{up}}+(1-q)\,V_{\text{down}}}{R}.\]
The real-world chance of going up does not appear, because you can build the option from stock and cash.
\end{idea}

\begin{example}[pricing a call]
$S_0=100$, moving to 120 or 90, with zero interest. Price a call with $K=100$.

\textbf{Solution.} Payoffs 20 and 0. $q=\frac{100-90}{120-90}=\frac13$. Price $=\frac{20}3\approx\mathbf{6.67}$.

\textbf{Replication check:} hold $\Delta=\frac{20-0}{120-90}=\frac23$ shares, and borrow 60. The value next period is $\frac23\cdot120-60=20$ or $\frac23\cdot90-60=0$. Cost today $=\frac23\cdot100-60=6.67$. \checkmark
\end{example}

\begin{why}[Why the real probability doesn't matter]
If anyone could quote a different price, you could buy the cheaper of the option and its replicating portfolio and sell the other, for a riskless profit in both scenarios. The real probabilities are already built into today's stock price.
\end{why}

\section{Arbitrage and odds}
Decimal odds $o$ imply a probability $\frac1o$. If the implied probabilities over all outcomes sum to less than 1, you can bet on all of them for a guaranteed profit, staking in proportion to $\frac1o$. Normally they sum to more than 1, and the excess is the bookmaker's margin.

\section{Risk-adjusted performance}
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{ll}
\toprule
Sharpe ratio & $\frac{\text{return}-\text{risk-free}}{\text{volatility}}$\\
Beta & $\beta=\frac{\text{Cov}(r,r_m)}{\text{Var}(r_m)}=\rho\frac{\sigma}{\sigma_m}$\\
CAPM & $\E[r]=r_f+\beta(\E[r_m]-r_f)$\\
Kelly fraction & $f^*=p-\frac qb$, for a bet paying $b$-to-1\\
Kelly growth rate & $g=p\ln(1+bf)+q\ln(1-f)$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\begin{exercises}
\item Two dice are rolled and you receive $\max(S-7,0)$, where $S$ is their sum. What is it worth?
\item $S_0=50$, moving to 60 or 40, with zero interest. Price the $K=50$ call and the $K=50$ put.
\item $S_0=100$, moving to 110 or 90, with $R=1.02$. Price a $K=100$ call.
\item A stock is at 100, a call with strike 105 costs 10, and the discount factor is 0.95. What should the same-strike put cost?
\item A bookmaker offers 1.9 on team A and 2.2 on team B (only two outcomes). Is there an arbitrage? If so, what is the guaranteed return?
\item A fund returns 15\% with 20\% volatility. The risk-free rate is 5\%. Find its Sharpe ratio.
\item A stock has Cov$(r,r_m)=0.02$ and Var$(r_m)=0.04$. With $r_f=4\%$ and $\E[r_m]=10\%$, find its beta and CAPM return.
\item For an even-money bet with $p=0.6$, find the Kelly fraction and the growth rate per bet.
\item What position has the payoff $S_T-K$? What does a long call plus a long put at the same strike (a straddle) pay? If the two cost 10 in total at $K=100$, where does it break even?
\item You may roll a die and either take the face value in rupees or pay \Rs1 to reroll, as often as you like. Find the value of the game.
\item A game: toss a coin until it lands tails. You receive \Rs$n$, where $n$ is the number of tosses. What is its fair price? What would you pay if the payoff were $2^n$?
\item A market is quoted at 49--51 on the number of heads in 100 tosses. Someone offers to buy from you at 51 after peeking at the first 10 tosses. What should you worry about?
\end{exercises}

%=====================================================================
\chapter{Maths Refresher for Quant Interviews}\label{ch:mathref}

Interviewers expect quick, reliable fluency with basic calculus and linear algebra.

\section{Approximations}
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\toprule
Rule & Example & True value\\\midrule
$(1+x)^n\approx1+nx$ for small $nx$ & $1.01^{5}\approx1.05$ & 1.0510\\
$\sqrt{a^2+b}\approx a+\frac b{2a}$ & $\sqrt{17}\approx4+\frac18=4.125$ & 4.1231\\
$e^x\approx1+x+\frac{x^2}2$ & $e^{0.1}\approx1.105$ & 1.1052\\
$\ln(1+x)\approx x-\frac{x^2}2$ & $\ln1.1\approx0.095$ & 0.0953\\
$\left(1-\frac1n\right)^n\approx\frac1e$ & $0.99^{100}\approx0.368$ & 0.366\\
Stirling: $n!\approx\sqrt{2\pi n}\left(\frac ne\right)^n$ & $10!\approx3.60\times10^6$ & $3.63\times10^6$\\
$\C{2n}n\approx\frac{4^n}{\sqrt{\pi n}}$ & $\frac{\C{100}{50}}{2^{100}}\approx0.0798$ & 0.0796\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\section{Calculus essentials}
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\toprule
$f(x)$ & $f'(x)$ & $\int f\,dx$\\\midrule
$x^n$ & $nx^{n-1}$ & $\frac{x^{n+1}}{n+1}$ ($n\ne-1$)\\
$e^{ax}$ & $ae^{ax}$ & $\frac{e^{ax}}a$\\
$\ln x$ & $\frac1x$ & $x\ln x-x$\\
$\frac1x$ & $-\frac1{x^2}$ & $\ln|x|$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
\item \textbf{Chain rule:} $(f(g(x)))'=f'(g(x))g'(x)$. \textbf{Product rule:} $(fg)'=f'g+fg'$. \textbf{Parts:} $\int u\,dv=uv-\int v\,du$.
\item \textbf{Log-differentiation} for $x^x$: $y=e^{x\ln x}$, so $y'=x^x(\ln x+1)$.
\item \textbf{Optimise:} set $f'=0$; a negative second derivative means a maximum.
\item \textbf{Gamma:} $\int_0^\infty x^ne^{-x}dx=n!$; $\quad\int_{-\infty}^\infty e^{-x^2}dx=\sqrt\pi$.
\end{itemize}

\section{Series}
\[\sum_{k\ge0}x^k=\frac1{1-x},\qquad\sum_{k\ge1}kx^k=\frac x{(1-x)^2},\qquad e^x=\sum_{k\ge0}\frac{x^k}{k!},\]
\[\ln(1+x)=\sum_{k\ge1}\frac{(-1)^{k+1}x^k}k,\qquad\sum_{k\ge1}\frac1{k^2}=\frac{\pi^2}6.\]
Harmonic sum: $H_n\approx\ln n+0.577$. So the sum diverges, but extremely slowly.

\section{Linear algebra essentials}
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
\item For a $2\times2$ matrix $\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}$: determinant $ad-bc$; inverse $\frac1{\det}\begin{pmatrix}d&-b\\-c&a\end{pmatrix}$.
\item Eigenvalues solve $\det(A-\lambda I)=0$. Their \textbf{sum is the trace} and their \textbf{product is the determinant}.
\item A covariance matrix is symmetric and positive semi-definite, so all its eigenvalues are real and at least 0. This gives $|\rho|\le1$.
\item $A^n=PD^nP^{-1}$ for diagonalisable $A$. So recurrences grow like the largest eigenvalue. Fibonacci comes from $\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$, whose largest eigenvalue is the golden ratio.
\end{itemize}

\begin{example}[which is bigger]
Which is larger, $e^\pi$ or $\pi^e$?

\textbf{Solution.} Compare $\frac{\ln x}x$ at $e$ and $\pi$: taking logs, $e^\pi>\pi^e\iff\frac{\ln e}e>\frac{\ln\pi}\pi$. The function $\frac{\ln x}x$ has derivative $\frac{1-\ln x}{x^2}$, so its maximum is at $x=e$. So $\mathbf{e^\pi}$ is larger. (They are $23.14$ and $22.46$.)
\end{example}

\begin{example}[integration by parts]
Evaluate $\int_0^1x\ln x\,dx$.

\textbf{Solution.} $\left[\frac{x^2}2\ln x\right]_0^1-\int_0^1\frac x2dx=0-\frac14=\mathbf{-\frac14}$.
\end{example}

\begin{exercises}
\item Differentiate $x^x$ and $e^{x^2}$.
\item Evaluate $\int_0^\infty x^2e^{-x}dx$.
\item Estimate $\sqrt{101}$ without a calculator, to 3 decimal places.
\item Estimate $1.02^{10}$ by the binomial approximation, then by $e^{0.2}$.
\item Find the eigenvalues of $\begin{pmatrix}2&1\\1&2\end{pmatrix}$.
\item Find the maximum of $xe^{-x}$ for $x>0$.
\item Find $\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(1+\frac2n\right)^n$.
\item Evaluate $\int_0^1x^2\,dx$, and use it to find $\E[U^2]$ and Var$(U)$ for $U\sim$ Uniform$(0,1)$.
\item Roughly what is the chance of exactly 500 heads in 1000 fair tosses?
\item Solve $x=1+\frac1x$ for $x>0$. What does this number have to do with Fibonacci?
\item Roughly what is $\ln(1000)$? Use $\ln2\approx0.693$ and $2^{10}\approx1000$.
\item Roughly how many people does a coupon-collector problem with 100 types need to complete a set?
\end{exercises}

%=====================================================================
\chapter{Brainteasers and Fermi Estimates}\label{ch:brain}

\section{Thinking habits}
\begin{idea}[What interviewers want to see]
\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
\item \textbf{Try small cases} to find a pattern. Then try to prove it.
\item \textbf{Work backwards} from the end state, as with the pirates and in strategy games.
\item \textbf{Look for invariants:} something that never changes, such as parity or a colouring.
\item \textbf{Change the point of view:} ants pass through each other; the fly's distance is just time $\times$ speed.
\item \textbf{Count the information:} each test or weighing has a limited number of outcomes.
\item \textbf{Talk through your reasoning:} a clean approach with a small slip beats a silent guess.
\end{enumerate}
\end{idea}

\begin{example}[invariant: the mutilated chessboard]
Remove two opposite corners from a chessboard. Can you cover the remaining 62 squares with $1\times2$ dominoes?

\textbf{Solution.} Every domino covers one black and one white square. Opposite corners have the same colour, so 32 of one colour and 30 of the other remain. \textbf{Impossible.}
\end{example}

\begin{example}[work backwards: a stone game]
Two players take turns removing 1, 2 or 3 stones from a pile of 21. Whoever takes the last stone wins. Who wins?

\textbf{Solution.} A pile of 4 is lost for the player to move: whatever they take, the other finishes. Likewise every multiple of 4. From 21, the first player takes 1 and leaves 20, and then always restores a multiple of 4. \textbf{The first player wins.}
\end{example}

\begin{example}[change of view: ants on a stick]
100 ants walk on a 1 m stick at 1 cm/s, in random directions. When two meet, both reverse. What is the longest time before all have fallen off?

\textbf{Solution.} Two ants bouncing look exactly like two ants passing through each other. So each ``ant identity'' walks straight on, and the longest is one walking the full length: $\mathbf{100}$ seconds.
\end{example}

\section{Fermi estimates}
\begin{idea}[Method]
Break the unknown into factors you can estimate. State assumptions clearly, round freely, and sanity-check the answer from another direction. Errors in factors tend to partly cancel. Being within a factor of 2 or 3 is usually enough.
\end{idea}
\begin{example}[smartphones sold each year in India]
\textbf{One approach.} Population about 1.4 billion. Perhaps 750 million own a smartphone. If a phone lasts about 3--4 years, about 200--250 million phones are replaced a year; adding first-time buyers gives somewhat more. Actual market figures are of order $1.5$--$2\times10^8$ phones a year, so the estimate has the right order of magnitude. A well-explained estimate matters more than the final number.
\end{example}

\begin{exercises}
\item \textbf{Bridge.} Four people with crossing times of 1, 2, 5 and 10 minutes must cross a bridge at night with one torch. At most two can cross at once, at the slower person's pace. What is the minimum total time?
\item \textbf{Ropes.} You have 2 ropes that each take 60 minutes to burn, at uneven rates. How do you measure 45 minutes?
\item \textbf{Horses.} 25 horses, a track for 5 at a time, no stopwatch. What is the minimum number of races to find the top 3?
\item \textbf{Pirates.} 5 ordered, perfectly logical pirates split 100 coins. The most senior proposes a split; if at least half (including the proposer) agree, it passes; otherwise the proposer is thrown overboard and the next proposes. What should the first propose?
\item \textbf{Lockers.} 100 lockers are closed. Person $k$ toggles every $k$th locker, for $k=1,\dots,100$. Which lockers end up open?
\item \textbf{Hats.} 100 prisoners stand in a line, each wearing a black or white hat, seeing all those ahead. From the back, each calls a colour. How many can be guaranteed to survive with a pre-agreed strategy?
\item \textbf{Two eggs.} With two eggs and 100 floors, what is the minimum number of drops, in the worst case, to find the highest safe floor?
\item \textbf{Poisoned wine.} One of 1000 bottles is poisoned, and the poison acts in about a day. How many test prisoners do you need to identify it in a single round?
\item \textbf{Jugs.} With a 3 L and a 5 L jug, how do you measure exactly 4 L?
\item \textbf{Bulbs.} Three switches outside a closed room control 3 bulbs inside. You may enter once. How do you match them up?
\item \textbf{Gold bar.} You must pay a worker one-seventh of a gold bar per day for 7 days. What is the minimum number of cuts?
\item \textbf{Wine and water.} Move a spoon of wine into a glass of water, stir, and move a spoon of the mix back. Is there more water in the wine or wine in the water?
\item \textbf{Missing number.} All numbers from 1 to 100 but one are given in random order. How do you find the missing one with very little memory?
\item \textbf{Handshakes.} At a party, prove that the number of people who shook an odd number of hands is even.
\item \textbf{Fermi.} Estimate the number of piano tuners in a city of 1 crore people, in the style of the example above.
\end{exercises}

%=====================================================================
\chapter{Famous Puzzles Collection}\label{ch:famous}

Quick-fire puzzles that appear in quant and consulting interviews. Try each before reading the solution at the end. Most have a short, clever argument -- find it.

\begin{exercises}
\item \textbf{Airplane seating.} 100 passengers board a plane with 100 seats, each with an assigned seat. The first passenger has lost their boarding pass and sits in a random seat. Each later passenger takes their own seat if free, otherwise a random free seat. Probability that the last passenger gets their own seat?
\item \textbf{Ants on a triangle.} Three ants sit at the corners of a triangle. Each picks a direction at random and walks along an edge. Probability that no two ants collide? What about 4 ants on a square?
\item \textbf{Ballot problem.} In an election, A gets 6 votes and B gets 4. The votes are counted in random order. Probability that A stays strictly ahead throughout the count? (Use the ballot theorem: $\frac{a-b}{a+b}$.)
\item \textbf{Socks.} A drawer contains red and blue socks. Two socks drawn at random are both red with probability exactly $\frac12$. What is the smallest possible number of socks? The smallest if the number of blue socks is even?
\item \textbf{100 prisoners.} Each of 100 prisoners may open 50 of 100 boxes looking for their own number. All must succeed. With the ``follow the cycle'' strategy (open the box with your own number, then the box numbered by what you found, and so on), roughly what is the success probability?
\item \textbf{Fair coin from a biased coin.} You have a coin with unknown bias $p$. How do you simulate a fair coin toss?
\item \textbf{Coprime integers.} Two positive integers are chosen ``at random''. Roughly what is the probability that they are coprime?
\item \textbf{Boy born on Tuesday.} Re-derive the $\frac{13}{27}$ answer from Chapter~\ref{ch:bayes} by listing what the 27 families look like.
\item \textbf{Die from a coin.} You have a fair coin. How do you simulate a fair 6-sided die as efficiently as possible? What is the expected number of tosses?
\item \textbf{Card colours.} A deck is shuffled. You turn cards over one at a time and may say ``stop'' at any moment; you win if the next card is red. Is there any strategy that beats $\frac12$?
\item \textbf{Three-way duel.} A, B and C hit with probabilities $\frac13$, $1$ and $\frac12$. They shoot in turn starting with A, and always at a target of their choice, until one survives. Should A aim at B, at C, or deliberately miss? Explain.
\item \textbf{Longest run.} Roughly how long is the longest run of heads in 1000 fair tosses?
\item \textbf{Birthday pairs.} How many people are needed for a better-than-even chance that someone shares \emph{your} birthday? Compare with the 23 in the classic problem.
\item \textbf{Six before odd.} What is the chance that a 6 turns up before an odd number, in repeated rolls of a die?
\end{exercises}
%=====================================================================
\part{Mock Tests and Formula Sheets}
%=====================================================================
\chapter{Mock Test 1: Screening Paper}\label{ch:mock1}
\textbf{25 questions in 30 minutes. No calculator.} Score $+1$ for a correct answer and $-\frac14$ for a wrong one. A score of 15 or more is solid for a typical screen. Use the error-log method from Chapter~\ref{ch:strategy} afterwards.

\begin{exercises}
\item 35\% of 240 plus 15\% of 120 is: (a) 96 (b) 102 (c) 108 (d) 112
\item A price rises 25\%. To keep spending fixed, consumption must fall by: (a) 20\% (b) 25\% (c) 30\% (d) 33\%
\item Cost \Rs400, marked \Rs600, discount 10\%. Profit is: (a) 25\% (b) 30\% (c) 35\% (d) 40\%
\item Compound interest on \Rs5,000 at 10\% for 2 years is: (a) \Rs900 (b) \Rs950 (c) \Rs1,000 (d) \Rs1,050
\item $A:B=2:3$ and $B:C=4:5$. Then $A:C$ is: (a) $2:5$ (b) $8:15$ (c) $3:5$ (d) $4:9$
\item Five numbers average 24. With one removed, the average is 22. The number removed is: (a) 28 (b) 30 (c) 32 (d) 34
\item A does a job in 10 days and B in 15. Together they take: (a) 4 (b) 5 (c) 5.5 (d) 6 days
\item A 200 m train at 72 km/h crosses a 300 m platform in: (a) 15 (b) 20 (c) 25 (d) 30 seconds
\item A boat at 8 km/h in still water goes 30 km downstream in a 2 km/h stream. It takes: (a) 3 (b) 3.75 (c) 5 (d) 7.5 hours
\item The LCM of 84 and 126 is: (a) 168 (b) 210 (c) 252 (d) 504
\item The unit digit of $7^{35}$ is: (a) 1 (b) 3 (c) 7 (d) 9
\item The remainder when $2^{31}$ is divided by 5 is: (a) 1 (b) 2 (c) 3 (d) 4
\item If $\alpha,\beta$ are the roots of $x^2-7x+12=0$, then $\alpha^2+\beta^2$ is: (a) 13 (b) 19 (c) 23 (d) 25
\item The sum of the first 20 terms of $5,8,11,\dots$ is: (a) 620 (b) 650 (c) 670 (d) 700
\item A rectangle has area 96 and length 12. Its perimeter is: (a) 28 (b) 32 (c) 36 (d) 40
\item A cylinder of radius 7 and height 10 has volume ($\pi=\frac{22}7$): (a) 440 (b) 770 (c) 1540 (d) 3080
\item The angle between the clock hands at 4:20 is: (a) $10^\circ$ (b) $15^\circ$ (c) $20^\circ$ (d) $25^\circ$
\item 1 January of an ordinary year is a Monday. Then 31 December is: (a) Sunday (b) Monday (c) Tuesday (d) Wednesday
\item The number of arrangements of the letters of LEADER is: (a) 360 (b) 480 (c) 600 (d) 720
\item A committee of 3 men and 2 women from 5 men and 4 women can be chosen in: (a) 40 (b) 60 (c) 84 (d) 126 ways
\item With two dice, the probability of a total of 9 is: (a) $\frac1{12}$ (b) $\frac19$ (c) $\frac16$ (d) $\frac5{36}$
\item The next term of 3, 5, 9, 17, 33 is: (a) 49 (b) 57 (c) 65 (d) 66
\item A person walks 6 km north and 8 km east. The straight-line distance from the start is: (a) 10 (b) 12 (c) 14 (d) 7 km
\item The pass mark is 40\%. A student scores 180 and fails by 20. The maximum marks are: (a) 500 (b) 550 (c) 600 (d) 650
\item 40 L of a mixture has milk and water in the ratio $3:2$. To make them equal, the water to add is: (a) 2 (b) 4 (c) 6 (d) 8 L
\end{exercises}

%=====================================================================
\chapter{Mock Test 2: Harder Paper}\label{ch:mock2}
\textbf{25 questions in 45 minutes. No calculator.} This is closer to a tough screen or a first-round quant test. Answers are numerical.

\begin{exercises}
\item How many positive divisors does 2520 have?
\item How many trailing zeros does $125!$ have?
\item Find the least number that leaves remainder 5 when divided by 6, 8 or 12, and is divisible by 7.
\item Find the remainder when $3^{100}$ is divided by 7.
\item Find the amount on \Rs10,000 for 1 year at 12\% compounded half-yearly.
\item A loan of \Rs11,000 is repaid in 2 equal annual instalments at 20\% compound interest. Find the instalment.
\item Goods are marked 30\% above cost, then sold at a 15\% discount. Find the profit percentage.
\item A, B and C do a job alone in 20, 30 and 60 days. A works every day and is helped by both B and C on every third day. How long does it take?
\item Three equal stretches are covered at 20, 30 and 60 km/h. Find the average speed.
\item Trains of 110 m and 90 m run in opposite directions at 40 and 32 km/h. How long do they take to pass?
\item In a 1 km race A beats B by 100 m, and B beats C by 100 m. By how many metres does A beat C?
\item If $\alpha,\beta$ are the roots of $2x^2-5x+3=0$, find $\frac1\alpha+\frac1\beta$.
\item How many integers satisfy $x^2-7x+10<0$?
\item Evaluate $\log_23\cdot\log_34\cdot\log_48$.
\item An infinite GP has first term 8 and sum 12. Find its ratio.
\item Find the inradius of a triangle with sides 9, 40, 41.
\item A cone has radius 6 and height 8. Find its curved surface area, in terms of $\pi$.
\item Find the area enclosed by $|x-2|+|y-3|=3$.
\item How many arrangements does BALLOON have?
\item How many positive integer solutions does $x+y+z=10$ have?
\item Factory A makes 40\% of output with 2\% defects, and factory B makes 60\% with 1\% defects. Given a defective item, find the probability it came from A.
\item What is the expected number of tosses of a fair coin until HT first appears?
\item Find the population standard deviation of 2, 4, 6, 8, 10.
\item $X\sim N(100,10^2)$. Roughly what is $P(X>110)$?
\item Revenue grows from \Rs400 crore at a 20\% margin to \Rs500 crore at an 18\% margin. Find the percentage change in profit.
\end{exercises}

%=====================================================================
\chapter{Formula Sheets}\label{ch:sheet}
\small
\section*{Numbers}
\begin{tabular}{>{\raggedright}p{5.4cm}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{9.6cm}}
\toprule
Divisors of $\prod p_i^{a_i}$ & Count $\prod(a_i+1)$; sum $\prod(1+p_i+\dots+p_i^{a_i})$; product $N^{d/2}$\\
Totient & $\varphi(N)=N\prod(1-\frac1p)$\\
HCF, LCM & $\text{HCF}\cdot\text{LCM}=ab$ for two numbers\\
Fermat, Euler & $a^{p-1}\equiv1\pmod p$; $a^{\varphi(n)}\equiv1\pmod n$\\
Unit digit & Cycle of length 1, 2 or 4\\
Legendre & Power of $p$ in $n!$ is $\sum_k\lfloor n/p^k\rfloor$\\
Digits of $N$ & $\lfloor\log_{10}N\rfloor+1$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}

\section*{Arithmetic}
\begin{tabular}{>{\raggedright}p{5.4cm}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{9.6cm}}
\toprule
Successive changes & $a+b+\frac{ab}{100}\ \%$\\
Undoing a change & $+\frac1n$ is undone by $-\frac1{n+1}$\\
Mark-up and discount & $\frac{MP}{CP}=\frac{1+p}{1-d}$; margin $=\frac{\text{markup}}{1+\text{markup}}$\\
False weight & Profit $=\frac{1000-w}{w}$\\
Interest & $\text{SI}=\frac{PRT}{100}$; $A=P(1+\frac R{100})^T$; $\text{CI}-\text{SI}=Pr^2$ (2 years), $Pr^2(3+r)$ (3 years)\\
Annuity, EMI & $\text{PV}=\frac Cr(1-(1+r)^{-n})$; $\text{EMI}=\frac{Pr(1+r)^n}{(1+r)^n-1}$\\
Perpetuity; CAGR & $\frac Cr$, $\frac C{r-g}$; $(\frac{\text{End}}{\text{Start}})^{1/n}-1$\\
Alligation & $\frac{\text{cheap}}{\text{dear}}=\frac{d-m}{m-c}$\\
Replacement & $V(1-\frac xV)^n$\\
Together time & $\frac{xy}{x+y}$; man-days: $\frac{M_1D_1H_1}{W_1}=\frac{M_2D_2H_2}{W_2}$\\
Average speed & Equal distances $\frac{2ab}{a+b}$; equal times $\frac{a+b}2$\\
After meeting & $\frac{S_1}{S_2}=\sqrt{\frac{t_2}{t_1}}$\\
Boats & $u=\frac{d+e}2$; $v=\frac{d-e}2$\\
Clock & Angle $=|30H-5.5M|$; hands meet every $65\frac5{11}$ min\\
Calendar & Odd days: year 1 (leap 2); 100 yrs 5; 200 yrs 3; 300 yrs 1; 400 yrs 0\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}

\section*{Algebra}
\begin{tabular}{>{\raggedright}p{5.4cm}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{9.6cm}}
\toprule
Quadratic & $\alpha+\beta=-\frac ba$; $\alpha\beta=\frac ca$; $D=b^2-4ac$; vertex at $-\frac b{2a}$\\
Power sums & $\alpha^2+\beta^2=s^2-2p$; $\alpha^3+\beta^3=s^3-3ps$\\
$a+b+c=0$ & $a^3+b^3+c^3=3abc$\\
AM--GM & $\frac{a+b}2\ge\sqrt{ab}\ge\frac{2ab}{a+b}$; $\text{GM}^2=\text{AM}\cdot\text{HM}$\\
Logs & $\log_ab=\frac{\log b}{\log a}$; $\log2\approx.301$, $\log3\approx.477$, $\log7\approx.845$\\
AP & $a_n=a+(n-1)d$; $S_n=\frac n2(\text{first}+\text{last})$\\
GP & $S_n=a\frac{r^n-1}{r-1}$; $S_\infty=\frac a{1-r}$\\
Power sums of naturals & $\frac{n(n+1)}2$; $\frac{n(n+1)(2n+1)}6$; $\left(\frac{n(n+1)}2\right)^2$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}

\section*{Geometry}
\begin{tabular}{>{\raggedright}p{5.4cm}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{9.6cm}}
\toprule
Triangle area & $\frac12bh$; $\frac12ab\sin C$; $\sqrt{s(s-a)(s-b)(s-c)}$; $rs$; $\frac{abc}{4R}$\\
Equilateral & $\frac{\sqrt3}4a^2$\\
Polygon & Angle sum $(n-2)180^\circ$; diagonals $\frac{n(n-3)}2$\\
Circles & Chord$^2$: $r^2=d^2+(c/2)^2$; $PA\cdot PB=PC\cdot PD$; $PT^2=PA\cdot PB$\\
Common tangents & $\sqrt{d^2-(r_1-r_2)^2}$; $\sqrt{d^2-(r_1+r_2)^2}$\\
Volumes & Cylinder $\pi r^2h$; cone $\frac13\pi r^2h$; sphere $\frac43\pi r^3$; frustum $\frac13\pi h(R^2+r^2+Rr)$\\
Surfaces & Cylinder $2\pi rh$; cone $\pi rl$; sphere $4\pi r^2$\\
Scaling & Lengths $k$; areas $k^2$; volumes $k^3$\\
Point to line & $\frac{|ax_0+by_0+c|}{\sqrt{a^2+b^2}}$\\
Trig & $\sin^2+\cos^2=1$; $c^2=a^2+b^2-2ab\cos C$; $\frac a{\sin A}=2R$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}

\section*{Counting}
\begin{tabular}{>{\raggedright}p{5.4cm}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{9.6cm}}
\toprule
Permutations & $^nP_r=\frac{n!}{(n-r)!}$; repeats $\frac{n!}{p!q!\cdots}$; circle $(n-1)!$; necklace $\frac{(n-1)!}2$\\
Stars and bars & Non-negative: $\C{n+r-1}{r-1}$; positive: $\C{n-1}{r-1}$\\
Derangements & $D_n$: 0, 1, 2, 9, 44, 265, 1854; $D_n\approx\frac{n!}e$\\
Onto functions & $\sum_k(-1)^k\C rk(r-k)^n$\\
Catalan & $\frac1{n+1}\C{2n}n$: 1, 1, 2, 5, 14, 42, 132\\
No two consecutive & $\C{n-k+1}k$\\
Grid & Rectangles $\C{m+1}2\C{n+1}2$; paths $\C{m+n}m$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}

\section*{Probability and statistics}
\begin{tabular}{>{\raggedright}p{5.4cm}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{9.6cm}}
\toprule
Bayes & $P(H\mid E)=\frac{P(E\mid H)P(H)}{P(E)}$\\
Linearity & $\E[\sum X_i]=\sum\E[X_i]$, always\\
Variance & $\E[X^2]-(\E X)^2$; $\text{Var}(aX+bY)=a^2\text{Var}X+b^2\text{Var}Y+2ab\text{Cov}$\\
Distributions & Bin: $np$, $npq$. Geo: $\frac1p$, $\frac q{p^2}$. Poisson: $\lambda$, $\lambda$. Exp: $\frac1\lambda$, $\frac1{\lambda^2}$. Unif: $\frac{a+b}2$, $\frac{(b-a)^2}{12}$\\
Coupon collector & $nH_n$\\
Conway & Pattern wait: $\sum 2^k$ over self-overlaps $k$\\
Gambler's ruin & Fair: $\frac iN$, duration $i(N-i)$; biased: $\frac{1-(q/p)^i}{1-(q/p)^N}$\\
Tower, Wald & $\E[X]=\E[\E[X\mid Y]]$; $\E[S]=\E[N]\E[X]$\\
Normal & 68--95--99.7; $z=1.645$ (5\% one tail), $1.96$ (2.5\% one tail)\\
CLT & $\bar X\approx N(\mu,\frac{\sigma^2}n)$; 95\% CI: $\bar x\pm1.96\frac s{\sqrt n}$\\
Kelly & $f^*=p-\frac qb$\\
Binomial pricing & $q=\frac{R-d}{u-d}$; price $=\frac{qV_u+(1-q)V_d}R$\\
Parity & $C-P=S_0-Ke^{-rT}$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\normalsize
\part*{Solutions}
\addcontentsline{toc}{part}{Solutions}
%=====================================================================
\chapter*{Solutions to Exercises}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Solutions to Exercises}
\small
Final answers are in \textbf{bold} at the start of each solution, so you can use this section as an answer key.
Solutions are grouped by chapter number. If your method differs but the answer matches, your method is probably fine: many of these have several good routes.

\section*{Part I -- Counting Fundamentals}

\solchapter{ch:fund}{Fundamental Principles}
\sol{1} \textbf{120.} Main: 6. Starter: 4 options or none $=5$. Dessert: 3 or none $=4$. $6\cdot5\cdot4=120$.
