\sol{2} \textbf{252.} Total 3-digit numbers: 900. With no 7: first digit 8 choices (1--9 except 7), others 9 each: $8\cdot9\cdot9=648$. $900-648=252$.

\sol{3} \textbf{1232.} First digit $\in\{3,4,5,6,7\}$, last digit odd, all distinct.
First digit odd (3, 5, 7): last digit is one of the other 4 odd digits; middle two: $8\cdot7$. $3\cdot4\cdot56=672$.
First digit even (4, 6): last digit any of 5 odd digits: $2\cdot5\cdot56=560$. Total $1232$. (8000 is even, so the upper bound doesn't matter.)

\sol{4} \textbf{243.} Each letter independently chooses a box: $3^5$. (The letters choose boxes, not the other way round.)

\sol{5} \textbf{819.} 1-digit: 9; 2-digit: $9^2=81$; 3-digit: $9^3=729$.

\solchapter{ch:perm}{Permutations}
\sol{1} \textbf{60; 40.} $\frac{6!}{3!\,2!}=60$. N's together: block [NN] $+$ B, A, A, A $\to\frac{5!}{3!}=20$. Not adjacent: $60-20=40$.

\sol{2} \textbf{309.} Sorted letters: E, H, M, O, R, T. Before M: E, H $\to 2\cdot5!=240$.
Fix M; next O -- smaller available: E, H $\to2\cdot4!=48$ (288).
Fix MO; next T -- smaller: E, H, R $\to3\cdot3!=18$ (306).
Fix MOT; next H -- smaller: E $\to 2!=2$ (308). Then E, R: nothing smaller. MOTHER is next: 309.

\sol{3} \textbf{30.} Need the last two digits divisible by 4: possible endings 04, 12, 20, 24, 32, 40.
Endings containing 0 (04, 20, 40): the other 3 non-zero digits fill the first three places: $3!=6$ each $\to18$.
Endings without 0 (12, 24, 32): the remaining 3 digits include 0, which can't lead: $3!-2!=4$ each $\to12$.

\sol{4} \textbf{277200.} E$\times3$, N$\times3$, G$\times2$, I$\times2$, R: $\frac{11!}{3!\,3!\,2!\,2!}$.

\sol{5} \textbf{7560.} A$\times4$, L$\times2$, H, B, D: $\frac{9!}{4!\,2!}$.

\sol{6} \textbf{2520.} By symmetry, half of $7!$.

\solchapter{ch:comb}{Combinations}
\sol{1} \textbf{66.} $\C{12}2$.

\sol{2} \textbf{110.} $\C53\C54+\C54\C53+\C55\C52=50+50+10$.

\sol{3} \textbf{54.} $\frac{12\cdot9}{2}$.

\sol{4} \textbf{127.} Mangoes 0--3 (4 choices) $\times$ $2^5$ for the fruits, minus the empty selection: $128-1$.

\sol{5} \textbf{570.} 10 odd and 10 even numbers. The sum is even iff the number of odd numbers is 0 or 2: $\C{10}3+\C{10}2\C{10}1=120+450$.

\sol{6} \textbf{136.} EXAMINATION: E, X, M, T, O once; A, I, N twice -- 8 distinct letters, 3 of them doubled.
All different: $\C84=70$. One pair $+$ 2 others: $3\cdot\C72=63$. Two pairs: $\C32=3$.

\solchapter{ch:circ}{Circular Arrangements}
\sol{1} \textbf{30240.} Block of 3 $+$ 7 others $=8$ units: $7!\times3!$.
\sol{2} \textbf{2520.} $7!/2$.
\sol{3} \textbf{72.} Total $5!=120$; together $4!\cdot2=48$.
\sol{4} \textbf{2880.} $4!\cdot5!$.
\sol{5} \textbf{(a) 96; (b) 144.} (a) 4 couple-blocks: $3!$, internal $2^4$: $6\cdot16$. (b) Men $3!$, women in gaps $4!$.

\solchapter{ch:dist}{Distributions}
\sol{1} \textbf{84.} $\C93$.
\sol{2} \textbf{286.} Pre-give: $a'+b'+c+d=10$: $\C{13}3$.
\sol{3} \textbf{280.} $\frac{9!}{(3!)^3\,3!}$.
\sol{4} \textbf{8.} All in one box: 1; split 3$+$1: 4; split 2$+$2: $\C42/2=3$. (Or $2^4/2$.)
\sol{5} \textbf{165.} $i+j+k+l=8$: $\C{11}3$.
\sol{6} \textbf{54.} $a+b+c=10$, $a\ge1$, all $\le9$. With $a'=a-1$: $\C{11}2=55$, minus the one case $a'=9$.
\sol{7} \textbf{10.} $\C82=28$ minus $3\cdot\C42=18$ (one variable $\ge4$).

\solchapter{ch:ie}{Inclusion--Exclusion}
\sol{1} \textbf{20.} $100-(60+45-25)$.
\sol{2} \textbf{286.} Multiples of 3: 166, of 7: 71, of 21: 23. $500-(166+71-23)$.
\sol{3} \textbf{540.} $3^6-3\cdot2^6+3$.
\sol{4} \textbf{78.} $120-(24+24-6)$.
\sol{5} \textbf{10.} $\C{17}2-3\C{10}2+3\C32=136-135+9$.

\solchapter{ch:der}{Derangements}
\sol{1} \textbf{44.} $D_5$.
\sol{2} \textbf{40.} $\C63D_3=20\cdot2$.
\sol{3} \textbf{455.} $6!-D_6=720-265$.
\sol{4} \textbf{1854.} $D_7=7D_6-1$ (using $D_n=nD_{n-1}+(-1)^n$).

\solchapter{ch:grid}{Geometry and Grids}
\sol{1} \textbf{210.} $\C{12}3-\C53=220-10$.
\sol{2} \textbf{150.} $\C62\C52$.
\sol{3} \textbf{50.} Total 120; sharing exactly one side with the decagon: $10\times6=60$; sharing two sides: 10. $120-70=50$. (Shortcut: $\frac{n(n-4)(n-5)}6$.)
\sol{4} \textbf{60.} $\C42\C52$.
\sol{5} \textbf{524.} $\C{12}6-\C63^2=924-400$.

\solchapter{ch:cat}{Catalan Numbers}
\sol{1} \textbf{14.} $n+2=6\Rightarrow C_4$.
\sol{2} \textbf{42.} $C_5$.
\sol{3} \textbf{42.} $C_5$.
\sol{4} \textbf{5.} $C_3$.

\solchapter{ch:pig}{Pigeonhole}
\sol{1} \textbf{25.} Worst case: 2 per month $=24$.
\sol{2} Boxes $\{1,10\},\{2,9\},\{3,8\},\{4,7\},\{5,6\}$; 6 numbers in 5 boxes force a full box, whose two numbers sum to 11. (With 5 numbers it can fail: $\{1,2,3,4,5\}$.)
\sol{3} \textbf{(a) 10; (b) 17.} (a) 3 of each colour $=9$, then one more. (b) All 9 green and 7 blue $=16$, then one more.

\solchapter{ch:numcount}{Number-Based Counting}
\sol{1} \textbf{3108.} $2!\cdot14\cdot111$.
\sol{2} \textbf{62.} $50+10+2$.
\sol{3} \textbf{26.} $15+7+3+1$.
\sol{4} \textbf{22.} $16+5+1$.
\sol{5} \textbf{20.} Units place: 10; tens place (30--39): 10.
\sol{6} \textbf{642.} $9+90\cdot2+151\cdot3$.

\section*{Part II -- Advanced Counting}

\solchapter{ch:rec}{Recurrences}
\sol{1} \textbf{81.} $a_n=a_{n-1}+a_{n-2}+a_{n-3}$, $a_0=1$: $1,1,2,4,7,13,24,44,81$.

\sol{2} \textbf{504.} A valid string starts with 0, 10 or 110, followed by a valid shorter string: $b_n=b_{n-1}+b_{n-2}+b_{n-3}$ with $b_0=1,b_1=2,b_2=4$: $7,13,24,44,81,149,274,504$.

\sol{3} \textbf{1224.} $a_n=2a_{n-1}+2a_{n-2}$, $a_1=3$, $a_2=8$: $22,60,164,448,1224$.

\sol{4} \textbf{37.} $1+\frac{8\cdot9}2$.
\sol{5} \textbf{127.} $2^7-1$.
\sol{6} \textbf{22.} $5^2-5+2$.
\sol{7} \textbf{41.} $f(6)=4\cdot11-3$.

\solchapter{ch:gf}{Generating Functions}
\sol{1} \textbf{140.} Coefficient of $x^{11}$ in $(1-x^6)^4(1-x)^{-4}$: $\C{14}3-4\C83=364-224$.

\sol{2} \textbf{40.} Case on \Rs10 coins: 0 tens $\to$ ways to make 20 from \{1,2,5\} $=29$; 1 ten $\to$ make 10 $=10$; 2 tens $\to1$.
(Making 20 from \{1,2,5\}: fives $=0..4$ leave $20,15,10,5,0$, with $11,8,6,3,1$ ways using 1s and 2s.)

\sol{3} \textbf{375.} $\C{23}3-4\C{14}3+6\C53=1771-1456+60$.

\sol{4} \textbf{25 (vs 27).} Coefficient of $x^6$ in $(1-x^6)^3(1-x)^{-3}$: $\C82-3=25$.

\sol{5} \textbf{6 each.} Distinct: 8, 7$+$1, 6$+$2, 5$+$3, 5$+$2$+$1, 4$+$3$+$1. Odd: 7$+$1, 5$+$3, 5$+$1$+$1$+$1, 3$+$3$+$1$+$1, 3$+$1$\times$5, 1$\times$8.

\sol{6} \textbf{16.} $(1+x+x^2)^4$ has coefficients $1,4,10,16,19,16,10,4,1$.

\solchapter{ch:burn}{Burnside's Lemma}
\sol{1} \textbf{51.} $\frac15(3^5+4\cdot3)=\frac{255}5$ (the 4 non-trivial rotations of a 5-cycle each have 1 cycle).

\sol{2} \textbf{39.} Add 5 reflections, each with 3 cycles ($3^3=27$): $\frac1{10}(255+135)$.

\sol{3} \textbf{57.} $\frac1{24}(3^6+6\cdot3^3+3\cdot3^4+8\cdot3^2+6\cdot3^3)=\frac{729+162+243+72+162}{24}$.

\sol{4} \textbf{140.} Identity $2^9=512$; $90^\circ$/$270^\circ$: centre $+$ two 4-cycles $\to2^3=8$ each; $180^\circ$: centre $+$ four 2-cycles $\to2^5=32$. $\frac{512+8+8+32}4$.

\sol{5} \textbf{4.} Identity: $\C63=20$. Rotations by 1, 5: none (would need one colour). Rotations by 2, 4 (two 3-cycles): one cycle red, one blue: 2 each. Rotation by 3 (three 2-cycles): red count would be even: 0. $\frac{20+2+2}6$.

\sol{6} \textbf{21.} Rotations: $81+3+9+3=96$. Reflections: 2 through opposite beads ($3^3=27$ each), 2 through edges ($3^2=9$ each): 72. $\frac{168}8$.

\solchapter{ch:bij}{Bijections, Identities, Stirling Numbers}
\sol{1} \textbf{5120.} $10\cdot2^9$.
\sol{2} \textbf{12870.} $\C{16}8$.
\sol{3} \textbf{495.} $\C{15-4+1}4=\C{12}4$.
\sol{4} \textbf{301.} $S(7,3)$.
\sol{5} \textbf{52.} Bell number $B_5=1+15+25+10+1$.
\sol{6} \textbf{50.} $c(5,2)$: split as 4$+$1: $5\times3!=30$; 3$+$2: $\C53\times2!\times1!=20$.
\sol{7} \textbf{42.} $p(10)$.
\sol{8} \textbf{1287.} Hockey stick: $\C{13}5$.
\sol{9} \textbf{50.} $\frac{10}{7}\C73$. (Same as Exercise~\ref{ch:grid}.3 -- triangles with no side on the decagon. Same problem in disguise.)

\solchapter{ch:mixcount}{Mixed Counting Practice}
\sol{1} \textbf{360.} [AAAA] $+$ L, L, H, B, D: $6!/2!$.
\sol{2} \textbf{84.} 0 can't appear (it would have to lead), so choose 6 of 1--9: $\C96$.
\sol{3} \textbf{3003.} Multisets of 6 digits from 1--9: $\C{14}6$.
\sol{4} \textbf{10.} 4 reds make 5 gaps; choose 3: $\C53$.
\sol{5} \textbf{262495.} $26^4-21^4$.
\sol{6} \textbf{35.} $\C84/2$.
\sol{7} \textbf{1568.} $64\cdot49/2$.
\sol{8} \textbf{2520.} $^7P_5$.
\sol{9} \textbf{630.} Choose the two digits $\C{10}2=45$; fill 4 places using both: $2^4-2=14$.
\sol{10} \textbf{377.} Fibonacci-type: $2,3,5,\dots$, the 12th term.

\section*{Part III -- Number Sense and Speed}

\solchapter{ch:speed}{Speed Maths and Estimation}
\sol{1} \textbf{9506.} Shortfalls 2 and 3: $98-3=95$, $2\cdot3=06$.
\sol{2} \textbf{4225; 13225.} $6\cdot7=42$; $11\cdot12=132$.
\sol{3} \textbf{1591.} $40^2-3^2$.
\sol{4} \textbf{123.} 16.67\% is $\frac16$; $738/6$.
\sol{5} \textbf{150000.} $25\times4=100$ and $48=4\times12$, then $12\times125=1500$.
\sol{6} \textbf{$\frac{15}{19}$.} Gaps from 1: $\frac29=0.222$, $\frac3{14}=0.214$, $\frac4{19}=0.211$. The smallest gap wins.
\sol{7} \textbf{10.} $2^{30}\approx10^9$ and a bit more: $1024^3\approx1.07\times10^9$, which has 10 digits.
\sol{8} \textbf{About 800.} $\frac12\cdot1200+\frac14\cdot800=800$. (Exact: $798.8$.)
\sol{9} \textbf{998001.} $(1000-1)^2$.
\sol{10} \textbf{It passes the check, and in fact it is correct.} $738\to0$, so the product must be $\equiv0$. $188928\to36\to0$. \checkmark

\solchapter{ch:numsys}{Number System}
\sol{1} \textbf{30; 2418.} $720=2^4\cdot3^2\cdot5$. $d=5\cdot3\cdot2$. $\sigma=31\cdot13\cdot6$.
\sol{2} \textbf{26.} Multiples of 2, 3 or 5: $50+33+20-16-10-6+3=74$. $100-74$.
\sol{3} \textbf{127.} HCF$(1651,2032)$; see Euclid above.
\sol{4} \textbf{548.} LCM$(12,15,20,54)=540$, plus 8.
\sol{5} \textbf{166.} A gap of 2 each time: LCM$(6,7,8)-2=168-2$.
\sol{6} \textbf{(15, 180) and (45, 60).} $xy=\frac{180}{15}=12$ with $x,y$ coprime: $(1,12)$ and $(3,4)$.
\sol{7} \textbf{1.} $\frac{36+63}{99}$.
\sol{8} \textbf{16.} LCM $=120$ s; $1800/120=15$ intervals, plus the start.
\sol{9} \textbf{No; no.} $437=19\times23$ and $221=13\times17$.
\sol{10} \textbf{$6^9$.} $36^{9/2}=6^9$, since $d(36)=9$.
\sol{11} \textbf{(a) 2; (b) 15.} $1800=2^3\cdot3^2\cdot5^2$. For a square, make the power of 2 even. For a cube, you need one more 3 and one more 5.
\sol{12} \textbf{4.} Exactly 3 divisors means the square of a prime: 4, 9, 25, 49.
\sol{13} \textbf{72 cm; 63 tiles.} In centimetres, HCF$(648,504)=72$. The floor takes $9\times7$ tiles.

\solchapter{ch:rem}{Remainders and Cyclicity}
\sol{1} \textbf{8.} $1234\to1$, $5678\to8$.
\sol{2} \textbf{2.} $5^6\equiv1$; $100\equiv4\pmod6$; $5^4=625\equiv2$.
\sol{3} \textbf{7.} $3^{2023}$: exponent $\equiv3$, unit digit 7. $7^{2024}$: exponent $\equiv0$, unit digit 1.
\sol{4} \textbf{41.} $(20+1)^{37}\equiv1+37\cdot20=741\pmod{100}$; higher terms contain $20^2=400\equiv0$.
\sol{5} \textbf{Yes to all three.} $2^2\equiv1\pmod3$, $2^4\equiv1\pmod5$, $2^3\equiv1\pmod7$, and 60 is a multiple of 2, 4 and 3.
\sol{6} \textbf{1.} $4!=24$ and all later factorials are divisible by 8. $1+2+6=9\equiv1$.
\sol{7} \textbf{59.} $n+1$ is a multiple of LCM$(2,\dots,6)=60$.
\sol{8} \textbf{$(10011100)_2$; $(234)_8$.} $156=128+16+8+4$.
\sol{9} \textbf{3.} $7\equiv-1\pmod4$, and the exponent is odd, so $-1\equiv3$.
\sol{10} \textbf{4.} $2^4\equiv1\pmod5$; $2026\equiv2$; $2^2=4$.
\sol{11} \textbf{219.} $10^{25}-7$ is 24 nines followed by a 3. $24\cdot9+3$.
\sol{12} \textbf{7.} $3^3=27\equiv3\pmod4$, so the unit digit is that of $3^3$.
\sol{13} \textbf{3.} $1+4+27+256+3125=3413$.
\sol{14} \textbf{3; no.} Since 4 divides 12, $n=12k+7\equiv3\pmod4$. Since 8 does not divide 12, the remainder mod 8 depends on $k$: 19 gives 3, and 7 gives 7.
\section*{Part IV -- Arithmetic Aptitude}

\solchapter{ch:pct}{Percentages}
\sol{1} \textbf{20\%.} $\frac{25}{125}$.
\sol{2} \textbf{+8\%.} $1.2\times0.9=1.08$.
\sol{3} \textbf{28\%.} $1-0.8\times0.9$.
\sol{4} \textbf{27.5\%.} $\frac{0.3\times1.1}{1.2}$.
\sol{5} \textbf{1050.} Net multiplier $1.4\times0.6=0.84$, a 16\% fall $=168$.
\sol{6} \textbf{500.} Pass mark $=178+22=200=40\%$.
\sol{7} \textbf{0.5 percentage points; 7.69\%.} $\frac{0.5}{6.5}$.
\sol{8} \textbf{90\%.} $1.2\times0.75=0.9$.
\sol{9} \textbf{55\%.} Fail in at least one: $35+25-15=45$.
\sol{10} \textbf{26,620.} $20000\times1.1^3$.
\sol{11} \textbf{\Rs5,78,000.} $800000\times0.85^2$.
\sol{12} \textbf{\Rs16 and \Rs20.} A 20\% fall buys $\frac54$ as much, so 5 kg is a quarter of the old quantity: old 20 kg, new 25 kg. $\frac{400}{25}$ and $\frac{400}{20}$.
\sol{13} \textbf{Down 1\%; a fall of 9.09\%.} $1.1\times0.9=0.99$. To undo $+\frac1{10}$, apply $-\frac1{11}$.

\solchapter{ch:pl}{Profit, Loss and Discount}
\sol{1} \textbf{\Rs900.} The swing from $-12\%$ to $+8\%$ is 20\% of CP $=180$.
\sol{2} \textbf{40\%.} $\frac{MP}{CP}=\frac{1.26}{0.9}=1.4$.
\sol{3} \textbf{25\%.} He gives 800 g for the price of 1000 g: $\frac{200}{800}$.
\sol{4} \textbf{50\%.} Per ``kg'' sold: revenue $=1.5\times0.8=1.2$ (units of cost per true kg); cost of the 800 g given $=0.8$. $\frac{1.2}{0.8}=1.5$.
\sol{5} \textbf{21\%.} CP per banana $\frac{10}{11}$, SP $\frac{11}{10}$: ratio $\frac{121}{100}$.
\sol{6} \textbf{38.8\%.} $1-0.8\times0.85\times0.9=1-0.612$.
\sol{7} \textbf{\Rs945.} CP $=840/1.2=700$; $700\times1.35$.
\sol{8} \textbf{20\%.} You pay for 4 and take 5, so $\frac15$ is free.
\sol{9} \textbf{\Rs56.} Target revenue $800\times1.26=1008$ from 18 kg.
\sol{10} \textbf{20\%.} Pre-tax price $=\frac{944}{1.18}=800$.
\sol{11} \textbf{\Rs500.} $\frac{660}{1.2\times1.1}$.
\sol{12} \textbf{37.5\%.} Per \Rs100 marked: buys at 80, sells at 110. $\frac{30}{80}$.
\sol{13} \textbf{20\%.} $\frac{0.25}{1.25}$.

\sol{1} \textbf{\Rs15,000.} $P(0.1)^2=150$.
\sol{2} \textbf{\Rs248.} $8000\times0.01\times3.1$.
\sol{3} \textbf{10\%; \Rs1,000.} $\frac{1331}{1210}=1.1$; $P=\frac{1210}{1.21}$.
\sol{4} \textbf{16\%.} Tripling needs 200\% in 12.5 years.
\sol{5} \textbf{12.68\%.} $1.01^{12}-1$.

\sol{8} \textbf{\Rs11,236 vs \Rs11,200.} $10000\times1.06^2$; half-yearly earns \Rs36 more.
\sol{9} \textbf{\Rs27,720.} $20000\times1.05\times1.1\times1.2$.
\sol{10} \textbf{10\%.} $1.61051=1.1^5$.

\solchapter{ch:ratio}{Ratio, Proportion, Partnership, Ages}
\sol{1} \textbf{3:5.} $A:B:C:D=3:4:\frac{14}3:5$ (scale $B:C=6:7$ to $4:\frac{14}3$, then $C:D=14:15=\frac{14}3:5$).
\sol{2} \textbf{\Rs3,600, \Rs2,400, \Rs1,800.} Ratio $6:4:3$ (sum 13), $7800/13=600$.
\sol{3} \textbf{15 and 25.} $\frac{3k+10}{5k+10}=\frac57\Rightarrow k=5$.
\sol{4} \textbf{\Rs17,000 each.} $40000\times12=480000$; $60000\times8=480000$.
\sol{5} \textbf{Son 15, father 45.} $3s+15=2(s+15)$.
\sol{6} \textbf{\Rs40,000 and \Rs50,000.} $4x-7y=5000$, $5x-9y=5000\Rightarrow x=2y$, $y=5000$, $x=10000$.
\sol{7} \textbf{A \Rs38,400; B \Rs21,600.} Salary \Rs6,000 leaves \Rs54,000, shared $3:2$: \Rs32,400 and \Rs21,600.
\sol{8} \textbf{40 km/h.} $v=\frac k{\sqrt w}$: $k=120$; $\frac{120}3$.
\sol{9} \textbf{A 35, B 15.} $A-5=3(B-5)$, $A+5=2(B+5)$.
\sol{10} \textbf{9.} $4:6=6:x$.
\sol{11} \textbf{30.} Value $=(5\cdot2+2\cdot3+1\cdot5)k=21k=210$, so $k=10$ and \Rs2 coins $=3k$.

\solchapter{ch:avg}{Averages}
\sol{1} \textbf{73.5.} $7\times\frac{21}2$.
\sol{2} \textbf{31.} The middle term is 27; the terms rise by 2 each.
\sol{3} \textbf{36.} $13\cdot32+13\cdot40-25\cdot36=416+520-900$.
\sol{4} \textbf{39.} New value $=$ old average $+17\times3$: $87=(x-3)+51$, so $x=39$.
\sol{5} \textbf{15.5.} $\frac{600+144}{48}$.
\sol{6} \textbf{31.} $A+B=80$, $B+C=86$, $A+B+C=135$, so $B=80+86-135$.
\sol{7} \textbf{\Rs36,000.} $15000+21\times1000$.
\sol{8} \textbf{3:2.} Alligation: $(85-76):(76-70)=9:6$.
\sol{9} \textbf{20.} $\frac{160-40}6$. (Removing two numbers that average 20 leaves the average unchanged.)
\sol{10} \textbf{90.} $75\times6-72\times5$.
\sol{11} \textbf{2 years.} The five were 85 in total, and are 100 today. Six people averaging 17 total 102.

\solchapter{ch:mix}{Mixtures and Alligation}
\sol{1} \textbf{7:3.} $(420-350):(350-320)=70:30$.
\sol{2} \textbf{4 L.} Milk 48 L must be 75\%: new total 64 L.
\sol{3} \textbf{81:19.} Wine left $80\times0.9^2=64.8$; water $15.2$.
\sol{4} \textbf{7:5.} Gold fraction $\frac79$ and $\frac7{18}$; equal weights: gold $\frac12(\frac79+\frac7{18})=\frac7{12}$.
\sol{5} \textbf{85 kg.} Total rises by $8\times2.5=20$.
\sol{6} \textbf{12 L.} Total cost $48\times60=2880$ must equal $48\times$(new volume): 60 L.
\sol{7} \textbf{\Rs4,000 at 6\%, \Rs6,000 at 9\%.} Mean rate 7.8\%: $(9-7.8):(7.8-6)=2:3$.
\sol{8} \textbf{60 L.} Milk stays 40 L; water must become 80 L, from 20 L.
\sol{9} \textbf{72.9\%.} $0.9^3$.
\sol{10} \textbf{30 L of 30\%; 10 L of 50\%.} $(50-35):(35-30)=3:1$.
\sol{11} \textbf{1:6.} A profit of $\frac16$ on cost: for each 6 units of liquid, 1 unit of free water.
\sol{12} \textbf{$6\frac23$ L.} Milk 15 L must become 10 L: $15\left(1-\frac x{20}\right)=10$.

\solchapter{ch:work}{Time and Work}
\sol{1} \textbf{21 days.} Rates 2 and 1 (units/day); total $3\times14=42$ units; A alone: $42/2$.
\sol{2} \textbf{15 days.} $\frac{T-5}{20}+\frac T{30}=1$.
\sol{3} \textbf{6 days.} 60 units; rates 5, 4, 3. Two days $\times12=24$; remaining 36 at rate 9: 4 days.
\sol{4} \textbf{24 hours.} $\frac16-\frac18=\frac1{24}$.
\sol{5} \textbf{8 days.} $15\cdot12\cdot8=20\cdot D\cdot9$.
\sol{6} \textbf{10 days; A alone 30 days.} $2(A+B+C)=\frac1{12}+\frac1{15}+\frac1{20}=\frac15$; $A=\frac1{10}-\frac1{15}$.
\sol{7} \textbf{13 min 15 s.} 60 units; rates 5, 4; each 2-minute cycle does 9. After 12 min: 54. A's minute: 59. B needs $\frac14$ min for the last unit.
\sol{8} \textbf{15 days.} A did $\frac4{10}$; B did $\frac6{10}$ in 9 days.
\sol{9} \textbf{4 days.} 1 man $=\frac53$ women; or 5 women $=$ 3 men, so the crew is $6+3=9$ men. 3 men take 12 days: $\frac{3\cdot12}9$.
\sol{10} \textbf{15 days.} $\frac{21}{1.4}$.
\sol{11} \textbf{15 days.} With 60 units, the rates are 3, 2, 1. Each 3-day cycle does $3+3+6=12$ units; 5 cycles.
\sol{12} \textbf{5760 L.} Leak rate $\frac C6$ per hour. $\frac C6-240=\frac C8$, so $\frac C{24}=240$.
\sol{13} \textbf{10 more.} 200 person-days did $\frac14$, so the job is 800 person-days. Remaining 600 over 20 days needs 30 people.
\sol{14} \textbf{120 L.} $\frac1{20}+\frac1{24}-\frac1{15}=\frac1{40}$ of the tank per minute must be what the drain takes away, so $\frac3C=\frac1{40}$.

\solchapter{ch:tsd}{Time, Speed and Distance}
\sol{1} \textbf{20 m/s; 90 km/h.}
\sol{2} \textbf{14.4 km/h.} $\frac{2\cdot12\cdot18}{30}$.
\sol{3} \textbf{48 minutes.} $\frac13$ of usual $=16$.
\sol{4} \textbf{60 km.} They meet in $\frac{72}{18}=4$ hours; the bird flies for 4 hours at 15 km/h. No infinite series is needed.
\sol{5} \textbf{6 km.} $\frac D5-\frac D6=\frac{12}{60}$.
\sol{6} \textbf{400 m; 3 minutes.} $\frac{0.1}{2}$ h is 3 min; $8\times0.05$ km.
\sol{7} \textbf{15 minutes.} $\frac{80-60}{80}=\frac14$ of each hour.
\sol{8} \textbf{3:4.} $\sqrt{\frac9{16}}$.
\sol{9} \textbf{Every 300 s; every 75 s; after 600 s.} $\frac{600}{2}$; $\frac{600}8$; LCM$(120,200)$.
\sol{10} \textbf{$\frac{180}{11}\approx16.36$ km/h.} $\frac3{\frac1{10}+\frac1{20}+\frac1{30}}$.
\sol{11} \textbf{80 km.} By the second meeting A has covered $3\times60=180$ km: 100 km to B's end, and 80 km back.
\sol{12} \textbf{50 km/h; 48 km/h.} Equal times give the arithmetic mean, equal distances the harmonic mean. Equal time is faster, because more of the distance is done at the higher speed.

\solchapter{ch:trains}{Trains, Boats, Races and Escalators}
\sol{1} \textbf{9 s.} $\frac{180}{20}$.
\sol{2} \textbf{72 km/h.} $\frac{600}{30}=20$ m/s.
\sol{3} \textbf{10 s.} $\frac{250}{25}$.
\sol{4} \textbf{84 km/h; 225 m.} $(v-3)\cdot10=(v+6)\cdot9$, so $v=84$. $L=81\cdot\frac5{18}\cdot10$.
\sol{5} \textbf{5 hours.} $\frac{36}{18}+\frac{36}{12}$.
\sol{6} \textbf{3:1.} $u+v=2(u-v)$.
\sol{7} \textbf{8 km.} $\frac D8+\frac D4=3$.
\sol{8} \textbf{19 m.} $0.9\times0.9=0.81$.
\sol{9} \textbf{32 m.} B runs $\frac35\times80=48$ m in A's time.
\sol{10} \textbf{36 s.} Rates add: $\frac1{60}+\frac1{90}=\frac1{36}$.
\sol{11} \textbf{1.5 km/h.} Rowing back also takes 20 minutes, by the frame argument. So the hat drifts for 40 minutes: $\frac{1}{2/3}$ km/h.
\sol{12} \textbf{2 hours.} Boats' speeds relative to the water add, and the current cancels: $\frac{60}{12+18}$.

\solchapter{ch:clock}{Clocks and Calendars}
\sol{1} \textbf{$100^\circ$.} $|210-110|$.
\sol{2} \textbf{$15^\circ$.} $|150-165|$.
\sol{3} \textbf{$21\frac9{11}$ and $54\frac6{11}$ minutes past 7.} $210-5.5M=\pm90$.
\sol{4} \textbf{Fast, gaining about $10\frac{10}{143}$ minutes a day.} A correct clock has hands meeting every $\frac{720}{11}$ of its own minutes. Here that takes 65 true minutes, a rate of $\frac{144}{143}$. Over 1440 min, the gain is $\frac{1440}{143}$.
\sol{5} \textbf{3:35.} $11{:}60-8{:}25$.
\sol{6} \textbf{10:48 am.} It shows 50 min per true 60. 240 shown is 288 true.
\sol{7} \textbf{Saturday.} 1600 years: 0. 1601--1900: 1 odd day. 1901--1999 is 99 years with 24 leap years: $123\equiv4$. Plus 1 for the date: $0+1+4+1=6$.
\sol{8} \textbf{Wednesday.}
\sol{9} \textbf{Friday.} 2026 is an ordinary year.
\sol{10} \textbf{2037.} Weekdays of 1 Jan: 2026 Thu, 2027 Fri, 2028 Sat, 2029 Mon, 2030 Tue, 2031 Wed, 2032 Thu (but leap), 2033 Sat, 2034 Sun, 2035 Mon, 2036 Tue, 2037 Thu.
\sol{11} \textbf{2/7.} 366 days is 52 weeks and 2 extra consecutive days. 2 of the 7 possible pairs contain a Sunday.
\sol{12} \textbf{3.} For example 2026 has three (Feb, Mar, Nov). More than 3 never happens: there are only 14 possible calendars, and a computer check of all of them gives a maximum of 3.

\section*{Part V -- Algebra}

\solchapter{ch:lin}{Linear Equations and Word Problems}
\sol{1} \textbf{36.} $a+b=9$, $9(b-a)=27$, so $b-a=3$.
\sol{2} \textbf{$x=3$, $y=2$.} Substitute $y=3x-7$.
\sol{3} \textbf{$k=4$.} $\frac36=\frac k8\ne\frac75$.
\sol{4} \textbf{30 of \Rs2, 10 of \Rs5.} All \Rs2 would be \Rs80. Each \Rs5 coin adds 3: $\frac{30}3=10$.
\sol{5} \textbf{3.} $7x$ must be divisible by 4, so $x=4,8,12$ with $y=18,11,4$.
\sol{6} \textbf{100.} All child tickets: \Rs6,000. Each adult adds 20: $\frac{2000}{20}$.
\sol{7} \textbf{$\frac14$.} $2(n+1)=d$ and $3n=d-1$, so $n=1$, $d=4$.
\sol{8} \textbf{$x=1,y=2,z=3$.} Subtract the third from the first: $2z=6$. Then $x+y=3$ and $x+2y=5$.
\sol{9} \textbf{35.} All correct $=150$. Each wrong costs 4: $\frac{150-90}4=15$ wrong.
\sol{10} \textbf{$x=7$, $y=3$.} $x-y=\frac{40}{10}=4$.
\sol{11} \textbf{8.} Adding: $6a+6b+6c=48$.
\sol{12} \textbf{2.} $8x+5y=100$ needs $x$ divisible by 5: $x=5,y=12$ and $x=10,y=4$. $x=0$ and $x=12.5$ are out.

\sol{3} \textbf{4.} $16-4k=0$.
\sol{4} \textbf{$6x^2-5x+1=0$.} Reverse the coefficients.
\sol{5} \textbf{13 and 14.}
\sol{6} \textbf{12 and 5.} $l+w=17$, $lw=60$.
\sol{7} \textbf{14; 52.} $16-2$; $64-12$.

\sol{9} \textbf{4, at $x=4$.} $(x-4)^2+4$.
\sol{10} \textbf{1560.} $13+(-8)+(-5)=0$, so the sum is $3\cdot13\cdot(-8)(-5)$.
\sol{11} \textbf{45 km/h.} $\frac{180}v-\frac{180}{v+15}=1\Rightarrow v^2+15v-2700=0$. (Check: 4 h vs 3 h.)
\sol{12} \textbf{Sum 6, product 6; roots 1, 2, 3.}

\solchapter{ch:ineq}{Inequalities, Functions and Optimisation}
\sol{1} \textbf{$x<2$ or $x>3$.}
\sol{2} \textbf{3.} $2<x<6$: 3, 4, 5.
\sol{3} \textbf{9.} $-1\le x\le7$.
\sol{4} \textbf{6.} $2x=3y=6$, so $x=3$, $y=2$.

\section*{Part VIII -- Logical Reasoning}

\solchapter{ch:rank}{Ordering, Ranking and Linear Arrangements}
\sol{1} \textbf{29.} $12+18-1$.
\sol{2} \textbf{14.} B from the left $=40-15+1=26$; $26-11-1$.
\sol{3} \textbf{42.} Passed $=7+28-1=34$; plus 5 and 3.
\sol{4} \textbf{28.} After the swap A is at B's old position, which is 17th from the left and 12th from the right: $17+12-1$.
\sol{5} \textbf{Arrangement M N L J O K. (a) K; (b) 3 (N, L, J); (c) L; (d) L.}
Since K $=$ J$+2$, J is at 1--4. M is at an end, with N next to M. Try cases: if M $=6$, then N $=5$, and J, K must fit with K $=$ J$+2$ among seats 1--4: (J, K) $=$ (1, 3) or (2, 4). L can't be at an end and can't touch K: with (1, 3), L must be at 2 or 4 -- both touch K; with (2, 4), the free seats are 1 and 3, L $=3$ touches K. So M $=1$, N $=2$; the free seats 3--6 take J, K, L, O with K $=$ J$+2$: (J, K) $=$ (3, 5) puts L at 4 or 6 -- 4 touches K, 6 is an end; (J, K) $=$ (4, 6) leaves 3 and 5: L $=5$ touches K, so L $=3$, O $=5$.

\solchapter{ch:circseat}{Circular Seating}
\sol{1} \textbf{Clockwise from A: A, E, F, C, D, B. (a) E; (b) F; (c) B.}
Facing outward, left $=$ anticlockwise ($-1$) and right $=$ clockwise ($+1$). Put A at 0 (seats 0--5 clockwise). C $=3$. B is A's immediate left: seat 5. D $=$ F$+2$ with F, D, E filling seats 1, 2, 4: the only option is F $=2$, D $=4$, leaving E $=1$. E's neighbours are A and F, so E is not next to C, as required.
(a) A's right $=$ seat 1 $=$ E. (b) Opposite seat 5 is seat 2 $=$ F. (c) Second to the left of E (seat 1) is seat $1-2=5$, which is B.

\sol{2} \textbf{2.} Going left there are 2 people between them (the other way, 4).

\sol{3} \textbf{V; W is second to the left of Q.} U is at seat 6, and right $=-1$: $6-3=3$, which is V. Q is at 5 and W at 7: W is two places clockwise from Q, i.e.\ second to Q's left.

\solchapter{ch:multi}{Multi-Attribute Puzzles}
\sol{1} \textbf{Mon: Uday (Macro); Tue: Tara (Risk); Wed: Ravi (Valuation); Thu: Sana (Equity); Fri: Veer (Credit).}
Veer and the Macro presenter (someone other than Veer) occupy Monday and Friday. Tara is neither, and not Wednesday, so Tara is on Tuesday or Thursday.
\emph{Tara on Thursday:} Ravi and Sana need consecutive days from the remaining slots. If they take Tue--Wed, Uday is the Macro presenter and must come before Tara, so Monday; Veer is Friday; Equity and Credit go to Ravi (Tue) and Veer (Fri) -- not consecutive, contradiction. If Ravi takes Monday (as Macro) and Sana Tuesday, Uday takes Wednesday and Equity/Credit fall on Tuesday and Friday -- contradiction again.
\emph{Tara on Tuesday:} Uday is before her, so Monday -- he is the Macro presenter; Veer is Friday. Ravi and Sana take Wed and Thu, in that order. Wednesday is Valuation (Ravi). Equity then Credit on consecutive days: Sana (Thu) Equity, Veer (Fri) Credit.
\textbf{Equity: Sana; Credit: Friday.}

\solchapter{ch:blood}{Blood Relations}
\sol{1} \textbf{Mother.} The only daughter of her mother is herself.
\sol{2} \textbf{Granddaughter.} A is C's daughter, and C is D's child.
\sol{3} \textbf{M is P's sister-in-law.} M is N's daughter; N is O's mother; so M is O's sister; O is P's husband.
\sol{4} \textbf{Brother.} Her paternal grandmother's only son is her father; his son is her brother.
\sol{5} \textbf{Wife.} R and Q are P's children; R is S's son; in a single family, S is the mother, i.e.\ P's wife.
\sol{6} \textbf{Mother.} K is L's wife; L is M's father; M is N's sister, so N is also L's child and K's child.

\solchapter{ch:syl}{Syllogisms}
\sol{1} \textbf{Only I.} All$+$No $\Rightarrow$ No manager is lazy. II contradicts ``No leader is lazy''.
\sol{2} \textbf{Only I.} Some$+$All $\Rightarrow$ Some apples are fruits. II is not forced.
\sol{3} \textbf{Only I.} Some funds are bonds, and no bond is a stock $\Rightarrow$ some funds are not stocks. II is not forced.
\sol{4} \textbf{Both.} Squares $\subset$ rings $\subset$ circles; II is the conversion of I.
\sol{5} \textbf{Either I or II.} A complementary pair with no definite link.
\sol{6} \textbf{Only I.} Cars $\subset$ bikes, and bikes and trucks are disjoint, so cars and trucks are disjoint -- II is impossible.
\sol{7} \textbf{Both.} Some$+$No gives I. Z must avoid Y, but X can extend outside Y far enough to contain all of Z.

\solchapter{ch:logic}{Formal Logic, Truth-Tellers and Weighing}
\sol{1} \textbf{It did not rain.} The contrapositive.
\sol{2} \textbf{No.} Something else may have cancelled it.
\sol{3} \textbf{Some bankers are not rich.}
\sol{4} \textbf{A is a knight, B is a knave.} If A is a knight, B is a knave, and B's claim of ``same type'' is false, which is consistent. If A is a knave, B is a knight, whose claim of ``same type'' would be true although they differ: a contradiction.
\sol{5} \textbf{II.} If exactly one is true, then exactly two are false, which is what II says.
\sol{6} \textbf{3; 4.} $3^3=27$, and 28 exceeds it.
\sol{7} \textbf{6; 4.} $1,2,4,8,16,32$ (sum 63, and five weights reach only 31); $1,3,9,27$.
\sol{8} \textbf{Only I.} ``Only'' makes II far too strong.
\sol{9} \textbf{Priya had a ticket. No:} a ticket is necessary, not sufficient, so it doesn't follow that a ticket-holder got in.
\sol{10} \textbf{The box labelled Mixed.} It is not mixed, so the fruit tells you what it really holds. Suppose it is an apple: that box is Apples. The box labelled Oranges cannot be Oranges and cannot be Apples, so it is Mixed. The last is Oranges.

\solchapter{ch:exp}{Expectation}
\sol{1} \textbf{91/36 $\approx$ 2.53.} $\min+\max=$ sum of the dice, so $\E[\min]=7-\frac{161}{36}$.

\sol{3} \textbf{53/5 = 10.6.} Expected non-aces before the first ace $=\frac{48}5$, plus the ace.
\sol{4} \textbf{5.5.} Runs $=1+$ (number of changes); each of 9 adjacent pairs differs with probability $\frac12$.
\sol{5} \textbf{21.} 6 rolls expected; the 5 non-six rolls average 3 (uniform on 1--5), giving 15, plus 6. (By Wald's identity.)
\sol{6} \textbf{$\approx$ 1.19.} $\C{30}2\cdot\frac1{365}=\frac{435}{365}$.

\solchapter{ch:fsa}{First-Step Analysis}
\sol{1} \textbf{14.} $2+4+8$.
\sol{2} \textbf{42.} $6+36$.
\sol{3} \textbf{10; 8.} HTH: $2+8$. HHT: only the full overlap, $8$.
\sol{4} \textbf{1/2; 25 rounds.} $\frac5{10}$; $i(N-i)=5\cdot5$.
\sol{5} \textbf{$\approx$ 0.116.} $\frac{1-1.5^5}{1-1.5^{10}}=\frac{-6.594}{-56.665}$.
\sol{6} \textbf{2/5.} Shift by 2: start at 2, absorbing barriers at 0 and 5: $\frac25$.
\sol{7} \textbf{THH, winning with probability 3/4.} Once a T appears, HHT can only appear after an HH that follows a T -- but then THH appears first. HHT wins only if the first two tosses are HH ($\frac14$).

\solchapter{ch:geo}{Continuous and Geometric}
\sol{1} \textbf{5/9.} $1-(\frac{40}{60})^2$.
\sol{2} \textbf{3/4.} The longer piece is uniform on $[\frac12,1]$.
\sol{3} \textbf{3/4.} $\frac n{n+1}$.
\sol{4} \textbf{1/2; $\pi/4$.} A triangle of area $\frac12$; a quarter disc of area $\frac\pi4$.
\sol{5} \textbf{1/2.} $\frac4{2^3}$.
\sol{6} \textbf{0.36.} Points more than 0.1 from every side form an inner square of side 0.8: $1-0.64$.
\sol{7} \textbf{5 minutes; 10 minutes.} With regular buses your wait is uniform on $[0,10]$. With Poisson buses you are more likely to arrive during a long gap, and memorylessness gives an expected wait equal to the full mean gap.

\solchapter{ch:stop}{Optimal Stopping and Games}
\sol{1} \textbf{14/3.} Keep the first roll if it is 5 or 6 (beats 4.25); keep the second if it is 4, 5 or 6 (beats 3.5); otherwise take the third. $\frac{11}6+\frac46\cdot\frac{17}4=\frac{14}3$.

\sol{2} \textbf{\Rs3; stop on 4 or more (stopping or continuing on a 3 are equally good).} Let $V$ be the game's value before paying for a roll: $V=-1+\E[\max(X,V)]$. Trying a threshold of 4: $V=-1+\frac{4+5+6}6+\frac36V\Rightarrow V=3$, which is consistent (you keep a roll exactly when it beats $V=3$).

\sol{3} \textbf{(a) 10\%; (b) 10\%.} (a) $0.55-0.45=0.10$. (b) $0.4-\frac{0.6}2=0.10$.

\sol{4} \textbf{Reject about 37; success probability $\approx$ 37\%.}

\sol{5} \textbf{10 losses; 1/1024; zero.} $1+2+\cdots+512=1023$ covers 10 losses. Ruin needs 10 losses in a row: $\frac1{1024}$. Expected profit per cycle $=\frac{1023}{1024}\cdot1-\frac1{1024}\cdot1023=0$.

\solchapter{ch:var}{Variance, Covariance and Correlation}
\sol{1} \textbf{$\frac{35}{12}\approx2.92$; $\frac{35}6\approx5.83$.} $\frac{91}6-\frac{49}4$. Variances add.
\sol{2} \textbf{30; 21; about 4.58.} $np$; $np(1-p)$.
\sol{3} \textbf{13.} $4\cdot4+9\cdot1-2\cdot2\cdot3\cdot1$.
\sol{4} \textbf{$\frac1{\sqrt2}\approx0.707$.} Cov$(X,X+Y)=\sigma^2$; divide by $\sigma\cdot\sqrt2\sigma$.
\sol{5} \textbf{$\frac67\approx85.7\%$.} $\frac{0.09-0.03}{0.04+0.09-0.06}$.
\sol{6} \textbf{1.5.} $\frac{15}{\sqrt{100}}$.
\sol{7} \textbf{$\frac{1225}{1296}=\left(\frac{35}{36}\right)^2\approx0.945$.} $\E[\max\cdot\min]=\E[XY]=\frac{49}4$; subtract $\frac{161}{36}\cdot\frac{91}{36}$.
\sol{8} \textbf{9.} $5+2^2$.
\sol{9} \textbf{9.} $\frac{30}{\sqrt n}=10$.
\sol{10} \textbf{$-1$.} $Y=10-X$ is an exact decreasing linear function of $X$.

\solchapter{ch:clt}{The Normal Distribution, LLN and CLT}
\sol{1} \textbf{About 2.3\%; about 68\%.} $z=2$; within one SD.
\sol{2} \textbf{About 68.} One SD above the mean.
\sol{3} \textbf{About 71\%.} SD 10. $z=\frac{10.5}{10}=1.05$; $1-2(0.147)$. (Exact: 70.6\%.)
\sol{4} \textbf{About 3.7\%.} Mean 350; SD $\sqrt{100\cdot\frac{35}{12}}\approx17.08$. $z=\frac{380.5-350}{17.08}\approx1.79$.
\sol{5} \textbf{4 times.} Error scales as $\frac1{\sqrt n}$.
\sol{6} \textbf{$55\%\pm4.9\%$.} $1.96\sqrt{\frac{0.55\cdot0.45}{400}}$.
\sol{7} \textbf{About 15.9\%; about 2.3\%.} $X+Y\sim N(30,25)$, so $z=1$. $X-Y\sim N(-10,25)$, so $z=2$.
\sol{8} \textbf{About 23.8\%.} $1.5\%\times\sqrt{252}$.
\sol{9} \textbf{Yes, just.} $z=\frac{60-50}5=2>1.96$.
\sol{10} \textbf{About 6.7\%; about 7.} $z=1.5$.

\solchapter{ch:tower}{Conditional Expectation and Recursion}
\sol{1} \textbf{12.25.} $3.5\times3.5$.
\sol{2} \textbf{7.} $2\times3.5$.
\sol{3} \textbf{$\frac12$.} $\frac{1/3}{1-\frac23\cdot\frac12}$.
\sol{4} \textbf{$\frac{11}4$.} $\frac{10+1}{3+1}$.
\sol{5} \textbf{$\frac23$.} Each draw has the same chance as the first, by the urn's symmetry.
\sol{6} \textbf{$\frac14$.} Each break halves the expected length.
\sol{7} \textbf{Mean \Rs2,000; SD about \Rs652.} Var $=10\cdot50^2+10\cdot200^2=425000$.
\sol{8} \textbf{$\frac14$.} $\E[U_2\mid U_1]=\frac{U_1}2$.
\sol{9} \textbf{1; 5.} Before the first 6, each of the faces 1--5 is as likely to come up as the 6 itself, so it occurs on average once.
\sol{10} \textbf{$\frac43$.} Per toss, $P(\text{heads})=\frac23\cdot\frac12+\frac13\cdot1=\frac23$. By linearity, $2\cdot\frac23=\frac43$.

\solchapter{ch:walk}{Random Walks and Markov Chains}
\sol{1} \textbf{$\pi=\left(\frac56,\frac16\right)$.} $0.1\pi_A=0.5\pi_B$.
\sol{2} \textbf{$\frac5{16}$.} $\frac{\C63}{64}$.
\sol{3} \textbf{6.} Mirror the start to $-2$. Then go from $-2$ to $+2$ in 6 steps: 5 ups and 1 down. (Total 15 end at $+2$.)
\sol{4} \textbf{0.6; 24.} $\frac6{10}$; $4\times6$.
\sol{5} \textbf{$\frac23$; $\frac8{27}$.} $\frac qp=\frac23$, and then cube it.
\sol{6} \textbf{$\frac13$.} A fair game: $\frac{10}{30}$.
\sol{7} \textbf{8.} A regular graph, so $\pi$ is uniform on the 8 vertices.
\sol{8} \textbf{16.} $4(8-4)$.
\sol{9} \textbf{$\frac14$.} $\frac{5-3}{8}$.
\sol{10} \textbf{25.} $5\times5$.

\solchapter{ch:trade}{Trading, Options and Pricing}
\sol{1} \textbf{$\frac{35}{36}\approx0.97$.} $S-7=1,\dots,5$ with counts $5,4,3,2,1$: $\frac{5+8+9+8+5}{36}$.
\sol{2} \textbf{5; 5.} $q=\frac12$. Payoffs 10 or 0. By parity, equal at $K=S_0$ with zero rates.
\sol{3} \textbf{About 5.88.} $q=\frac{1.02-0.9}{0.2}=0.6$. $\frac{0.6\cdot10}{1.02}$.
\sol{4} \textbf{9.75.} $P=C-S_0+Kd=10-100+99.75$.
\sol{5} \textbf{Yes; about 1.95\%.} $\frac1{1.9}+\frac1{2.2}\approx0.981<1$. Return $\frac1{0.981}-1$.
\sol{6} \textbf{0.5.} $\frac{15-5}{20}$.
\sol{7} \textbf{0.5; 7\%.} $4+0.5\cdot6$.
\sol{8} \textbf{20\%; about 2.0\% (log) per bet.} $0.6-0.4$. $0.6\ln1.2+0.4\ln0.8\approx0.0201$.
\sol{9} \textbf{Long call plus short put; $|S_T-K|$; 90 and 110.}
\sol{10} \textbf{\Rs4; keep 3 or more (a 3 exactly ties), and reroll 1 or 2.} Let $V$ be the value. You keep $x$ whenever $x\ge V-1$. Trying a threshold of 3: $V=\frac{3+4+5+6}6+\frac26(V-1)$, so $V=4$, and $V-1=3$ is consistent.
\sol{11} \textbf{\Rs2; no finite fair price.} $\E[n]=2$. The second is the St Petersburg game, with infinite expectation.
\sol{12} \textbf{Adverse selection.} Their purchase hints that the first 10 had more heads than the expected 5. Your fair value should move up, and so should your quote.

\solchapter{ch:mathref}{Maths Refresher}
\sol{1} \textbf{$x^x(\ln x+1)$; $2xe^{x^2}$.}
\sol{2} \textbf{2.} $2!$.
\sol{3} \textbf{10.05.} $10+\frac1{20}$. (True: 10.0499.)
\sol{4} \textbf{1.2; 1.2214.} True: 1.2190.
\sol{5} \textbf{3 and 1.} Trace 4, determinant 3.
\sol{6} \textbf{$\frac1e$, at $x=1$.} $f'=(1-x)e^{-x}$.
\sol{7} \textbf{$e^2$.}
\sol{8} \textbf{$\frac13$; $\frac13$; $\frac1{12}$.} $\frac13-\frac14$.
\sol{9} \textbf{About 2.5\%.} $\frac1{\sqrt{\pi\cdot500}}\approx0.0252$.
\sol{10} \textbf{$\frac{1+\sqrt5}2\approx1.618$.} The ratio of successive Fibonacci numbers tends to it.
\sol{11} \textbf{About 6.9.} $10\times0.693$.
\sol{12} \textbf{About 520.} $100H_{100}\approx100(\ln100+0.577)\approx518$.

\solchapter{ch:brain}{Brainteasers and Fermi Estimates}
\sol{1} \textbf{17 minutes.} 1 and 2 cross (2); 1 returns (1); 5 and 10 cross (10); 2 returns (2); 1 and 2 cross (2). The idea is to get the two slowest over together.
\sol{2} Light rope A at both ends and rope B at one end. A finishes in 30 minutes. At that moment, light B's other end: its remaining 30 minutes takes 15 more.
\sol{3} \textbf{7.} 5 heats, then a race of the 5 winners (6 races). For the seventh, race whoever could still be 2nd or 3rd: the 2nd and 3rd of the winners' race, the 2nd and 3rd in the champion's heat, and the 2nd in the heat of the runner-up.
\sol{4} \textbf{98, 0, 1, 0, 1.} Backwards. With 2 pirates, the senior takes all (needs just their own vote). With 3: $(99,0,1)$. With 4: $(99,0,1,0)$. With 5: $(98,0,1,0,1)$. Each pirate is bought for one more than they get next round.
\sol{5} \textbf{The perfect squares: 10 lockers.} A locker is toggled once per divisor, and only squares have an odd number of divisors.
\sol{6} \textbf{99.} The last in line calls the parity of the black hats they see (a 50\% chance for them). Each person ahead can then deduce their own hat.
\sol{7} \textbf{14.} Drop at floors $14, 27, 39,\dots$, with gaps that fall by 1. You need the smallest $k$ with $\frac{k(k+1)}2\ge100$.
\sol{8} \textbf{10.} Number the bottles in binary. Prisoner $j$ drinks from each bottle with bit $j$ set. The deaths spell out the bottle's number, and $2^{10}=1024\ge1000$.
\sol{9} Fill 5, pour into 3 (leaves 2 in the 5). Empty the 3. Pour the 2 into the 3. Fill 5, and top up the 3 with 1 more. That leaves 4 in the 5.
\sol{10} Turn switch 1 on for ten minutes, then off. Turn switch 2 on, and go in. The lit bulb is 2; the warm dark bulb is 1; the cold dark bulb is 3.
\sol{11} \textbf{2 cuts.} Pieces of 1, 2 and 4 sevenths, with change given back. It is binary again.
\sol{12} \textbf{Equal.} Each glass ends with its original volume, so whatever wine left the wine glass was replaced by an equal amount of water.
\sol{13} Subtract the sum of the given numbers from 5050. Alternatively, XOR all of them against $1,\dots,100$, avoiding overflow.
\sol{14} Each handshake adds 2 to the total of everybody's counts, so that total is even. Even-count people contribute an even total, so the odd-count people must also total an even number, and only an even number of odd values can do that.
\sol{15} \textbf{Tens to a few hundred.} For example: 1 crore people is about 25 lakh homes. If 1 in 200 has a piano, that is 12,500 pianos, and each may be tuned once a year. A tuner might do 2 a day for 250 days, or 500 a year. That gives about $\mathbf{25}$ tuners. A much lower ownership rate in India would make this lower still. Any answer with clear assumptions is fine.
\solchapter{ch:famous}{Famous Puzzles}
\sol{1} \textbf{1/2.} The process ends when someone takes either seat 1 or seat 100, and at every step these two are equally likely to be chosen. So the last passenger finds seat 100 free exactly when seat 1 was taken first: probability $\frac12$. (The answer is the same for any number of passengers $\ge2$.)
\sol{2} \textbf{1/4; 1/8.} No collision only if all ants go the same way: $\frac2{2^3}$ and $\frac2{2^4}$.
\sol{3} \textbf{1/5.} $\frac{6-4}{10}$.
\sol{4} \textbf{4 socks (3 red, 1 blue); 21 socks (15 red, 6 blue).} Need $\frac{r(r-1)}{(r+b)(r+b-1)}=\frac12$; check $\frac34\cdot\frac23=\frac12$ and $\frac{15}{21}\cdot\frac{14}{20}=\frac12$.
\sol{5} \textbf{$\approx$ 31\%.} All succeed iff the random permutation has no cycle longer than 50: $1-\sum_{k=51}^{100}\frac1k\approx1-\ln2\approx0.31$. (Opening boxes at random would give $2^{-100}$.)
\sol{6} Toss twice: HT $\to$ ``heads'', TH $\to$ ``tails'', HH or TT $\to$ repeat. HT and TH both have probability $p(1-p)$. (Von Neumann's trick.)
\sol{7} \textbf{$6/\pi^2\approx0.61$.} A prime $p$ divides both with probability $\frac1{p^2}$, so $P=\prod_p(1-\frac1{p^2})=\frac1{\zeta(2)}=\frac6{\pi^2}$.
\sol{8} Families with the older child a Tuesday-boy: 14 (younger child any type). With the younger child a Tuesday-boy: 14. Both: 1. Total 27. Both boys: $7+7-1=13$.
\sol{9} \textbf{4 tosses by the simple method.} Toss 3 times to get a number from 0 to 7. If it is 6 or 7, repeat. Each round works with probability $\frac34$, so the expected number of rounds is $\frac43$, and thus $4$ tosses. (A cleverer scheme reuses the leftover randomness and takes $\frac{11}3$.)
\sol{10} \textbf{No.} The fraction of reds left is a martingale, so every stopping rule wins exactly $\frac12$. Equivalently, saying ``stop'' and winning on the next card has the same chance as winning on the \emph{last} card, which is $\frac12$.
\sol{11} \textbf{Deliberately miss.} If A hits C, then B shoots A dead. If A hits B, then A faces C with C shooting first, and wins only $\frac14$ of the time; overall, aiming at B gives $\frac{11}{36}$. If A misses, B (certain) shoots C, the stronger threat, and A gets the first shot at B: a duel in which A wins $\frac13$ at once and otherwise dies. Missing gives A a win chance of $\frac13$, the best available.
\sol{12} \textbf{About 9 or 10.} $\log_2(1000)\approx10$. There are about 500 runs of heads, and each is longer than $k$ with probability $2^{-k}$.
\sol{13} \textbf{253.} $1-\left(\frac{364}{365}\right)^n>\frac12$ needs $n\ge253$. Interestingly, this is $\C{23}2$, the number of pairs among 23 people.
\sol{14} \textbf{$\frac14$.} Only rolls of 1, 3, 5 or 6 matter, and 6 is one of those 4.
